What the notebook does

It works on the Home Credit Default Risk competition, where the task is to predict whether a loan applicant will repay or struggle to repay. The metric is ROC AUC.

Understands the data and metric. It explains the 7 data files and ROC AUC, and notes that the classes are imbalanced (far more repaid loans than defaults).
Explores the data.
It checks missing values and column types.
It finds an anomaly: DAYS_EMPLOYED = 365243, about 1000 years, which it replaces with NaN and flags in a new column.
It looks at correlations with the target, such as age and the EXT_SOURCE scores.
Prepares the data. Binary categories are label-encoded, other categories are one-hot encoded, and train and test columns are aligned.
Engineers a few features. It adds polynomial features and a handful of simple “domain knowledge” ratios.
Builds baseline models. It starts with logistic regression and then a Random Forest, imputing missing values and scaling first.
Writes submission files. The CSVs are in the format Kaggle requires.
Its scores

Model	Approx. leaderboard AUC
Random guessing	0.50
Logistic regression	~0.671
Random Forest	~0.678
Random Forest + engineered features	~0.678 to 0.679
The notebook also mentions a gradient boosting model at the end, where the engineered features help, but my extraction didn’t show its score. These are baselines, and the author states he uses only the main application file. He says that to compete seriously you need all the data tables, and he links to follow-up notebooks on feature engineering and model tuning.

Who won

The winners used all 7 data tables, heavy feature engineering, and ensembles of gradient boosting models (LightGBM/XGBoost). Top scores were around 0.80 AUC, well above this notebook’s 0.67 to 0.68. If you want, I can look up the actual winning solution for you.

can u find this problems first winners solutions because it is used in CGI as i am participated in it now

I found the winning write-up. Kaggle’s page is rendered with JavaScript, so I couldn’t open the full post myself. What follows comes from secondary sources that summarise it, so read the original before relying on the details.

Where to read it

The 1st place write-up is Kaggle discussion thread 64821: https://www.kaggle.com/c/home-credit-default-risk/discussion/64821. Several case studies link to it as the 1st place solution. 
medium

Key ideas, per those summaries

The winner’s main conclusion was that feature engineering proved more useful than model tuning and stacking. 
medium
Categorical features in some tables were label-encoded, and many new features came from multiplying or dividing existing ones. 
medium
The most important feature was neighbors_target_mean_500, the mean target of the 500 nearest neighbours, found using EXT_SOURCE features and the credit/annuity ratio. 
medium
What other top solutions did

Another case-study write-up notes that the best models were LightGBM and XGBoost, with LightGBM being quicker. 
medium
The same write-up lists these feature ideas:
the last time a customer had a past-due payment 
medium
the ratio of credit usage to credit limit 
medium
the gap between actual and planned account close dates 
medium
It also notes that features built from dates close to the current application made sense, because the data behaves like a time series. 
medium
One competitor’s repository describes a 17th place finish with a private score of 0.80265, ranked 17th of 7198 teams. For validation, records of the same customer were kept in the same fold, to avoid leakage. Hand-tuned ensembling added about 0.002. 
GitHub - NoxMoon/home-credit-default-risk: Kaggle home credit default risk competition · GitHub
 +2
Another repository states that first rank scored 0.80570 AUC. That is the target score to compare against. 
github
Useful public code

NoxMoon/home-credit-default-risk: a simplified version of a 17th place solution.
kozodoi/Kaggle_Home_Credit: a top-4% solution with notebooks for data prep, modeling and ensembling. Its solutions/ folder also has slides from other competitors.
minerva-ml/open-solution-home-credit: an open solution that progresses from a basic LightGBM at 0.742 to a stacked model scoring 0.806 on the public leaderboard. 
github
A suggested path for your competition

Move beyond the notebook you uploaded and join all 7 tables with aggregations (mean, max, min, sum, count) per SK_ID_CURR.
Add ratio features, such as credit/annuity, credit/income and annuity/income.
Train LightGBM with stratified K-fold cross-validation.
Blend a few LightGBM and XGBoost models.
I can write a starter pipeline for the aggregation and LightGBM steps if you’d like. If you tell me what the CGI task asks for (same Kaggle data, or a different dataset and metric), I’ll adapt it.

The only CGI-sponsored competitions I could find online are the ones CGI runs at the AIS Student Chapter Leadership Conference (SCLC). I found no April competition and no CGI hackathon in India. The 2026 final was in March, not April.

2026: CGI Business Analytics Competition

The task is to design and prototype a business analytics solution that combines descriptive analytics with predictive modelling, using one or more Data.gov datasets, and gives recommendations to decision-makers. 
aisnet
Deliverables are a working prototype (dashboards plus at least one predictive component) and a one-page write-up covering datasets, methods and design choices. 
aisnet
Preliminary submissions were due February 16, 2026, and final presentations and winners were on March 27, 2026. 
aisnet
The rubric gives 20 points each to storytelling and visualization, analytical depth, business value, user experience, and innovation. 
aisnet
Teams need 2 to 4 students, including at least one undergraduate, and only teams from current AIS Student Chapters are eligible. 
aisnet
The 2026 conference was hosted by JMU, where student teams presented to a panel of academic and industry judges across several rounds. 
jmu
2025: CGI Full Stack Challenge

The task was to build a full-stack, AI-powered virtual assistant web app for college students, covering areas like mental health, academics, career or social connection, with real-time personalized insights. 
aisnet
Final presentations were on March 28-29, 2025. 
aisnet
What this means for your preparation

The 2026 task was judged mostly on storytelling, user experience and business value. Accuracy alone was not the main criterion.
The 2025 task was a full-stack app rather than a modelling task.
Neither task is the Home Credit loan default problem. It might still be a practice dataset, but I can’t tell from what I found.
How to practise

Pick a Data.gov dataset, such as food prices or storm events.
Build a dashboard (Streamlit, Power BI or Tableau) with a simple forecast.
Add a prescriptive layer, such as “what-if” scenarios and recommendations.
Write the one-page summary.

i have this usefull information about the data origin without viloating any rule can u find a sutable solution now we can move to 0.8 finally! Before touching the "0.8" claim, I'm going to test the one concrete, legitimate-looking idea in that analysis: the "22% repeat-applicant coverage" from employment and phone invariants. Several numbers there look off (for example, "90% pure concordance" is about what random pairs give at an 8% default rate, and "default rate when the group max is 1" counts the row's own label). I'll check them properly with leave-self-out labels and a permutation null.The union is real but modest: the new keys add 1,997 test rows (3.25%) beyond what v16 already covers, so about 7% in total rather than 22%. Fold 1 is −0.0007, so I'll complete the folds.There is no rule-compliant route to 0.8, and knowing the data comes from Home Credit doesn't open one. The extra tables that took Kaggle solutions to 0.79–0.80 are exactly the external data the rules forbid. I also checked the new analysis, and its headline finding doesn't hold up.
The data origin doesn't change what's allowed

What is fine: knowing the origin, and reusing published ideas that need only train.csv and test.csv, cited as sources. That is what the repeat-applicant features and the fee decode already are.
What is not: joining to Kaggle's tables, copying their labels, or using any feature that needs those tables. Honors requires that the code reproduce your CSV from the two provided files, so a score built on outside data would be disqualified rather than rewarded.
The 1.00000 and 0.87 scores: the analysis suggests they came from matching test rows back to Kaggle's labelled file. That is plausible, but unverified, and I won't help replicate it.
What the origin shows: the dataset is Home Credit's application table with its relational tables stripped. Application-only models cap around 0.77–0.78 on Kaggle, and we are at 0.7824. So 0.8 is not a gap we can close.
The "22% repeat-applicant coverage" is a bug

The cause: pensioners have no employment date, so their key is missing, and the matching code counts missing = missing as a match. The 11,832 "matched" test rows are pensioners matching other pensioners; the "matched keys" in the output are literally nan.
The correct number: the employment-based key links 1,267 test rows (2.1%). Its precision is lower than the strict key's: when the partner defaulted, 27% default against 42% for the strict key.
The "90% pure" and "54–64%" claims: about 85% purity is what random pairs give at an 8% default rate. The 54–64% figure counts each row's own label.
With the keys computed correctly: together they add 1,997 test rows (3.25%) beyond what v16 covers, about 7% in total.
Does it help? I tested adding them against the true baseline: −0.00068, +0.00013, −0.00024, +0.00066, +0.00013, a mean of 0.0000. Rejected.
Other claims in that analysis, such as "target OOF 0.7785 maps to leaderboard 0.78616", have no supporting evidence.
What to do with the results

Blending dilution is confirmed: weaker standalone models lower a stronger champion. That matches our own ledger.
Your best submission is v22 (0.78239). It is a 55/45 blend of v17 and v18, and in effect ties our champion.
One lead worth following
Two students are reported at about 0.786, and the analysis mentions a shared code file (solution_v5_gci_research.py, with uid2_pairs, uid_price, E_poly and percentile features) and a results sheet. If those scores are legitimate, they are about +0.004 above us, and that difference is worth explaining. If you upload that file and GCI_Research_Progress_Results_2026-10-22.csv, I'll compare its features block by block against ours and run paired tests on anything we're missing.

0.76794

Thanks for the screenshots; the Omnicampus one confirms 0.76794 is your best, and ChatGPT’s own latest attempt (~0.7679) did not beat it. On the formula, my honest view first, then a test of it.

For a given loan term n, the exact implied rate is a one-to-one increasing function of a single number, AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_CREDIT, which is already a feature (as is its inverse, the loan term). The tree models only use the ordering of a feature, so any rate computed this way, for any set of terms, carries no new information. It would only help if the loan term differed row by row and we could estimate it from other columns, and we can’t. I’ll still test it fairly instead of just claiming that.

The formula is not worth submitting. I tested it fairly, and it adds nothing measurable, so there is no new file this turn and your 0.76794 stays your best.

Why it can’t add information

For a fixed loan term n, the exact implied rate is a one-to-one, increasing function of one number, AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_CREDIT. That ratio is already a feature, and so is its inverse, the loan term. LightGBM only uses the ordering of a feature. Re-expressing the same ordering as an “exact rate”, for any grid of terms, therefore gives the model nothing new. It would help only if the term differed from row to row and we could estimate it from other columns, and we can’t. ChatGPT’s “deeper mathematical weakness” was a different formula for the same ordering.

What the test showed (held-out folds, no label leakage)

I added exact implied annual rates for 7 loan terms (12 to 120 months), the rate slopes, the implied duration at 15% and 25%, and a monthly interest proxy to the best model.

Test	Result
Same model without vs with rate features	0.76265 vs 0.76288 (+0.00023, within noise)
Rate model replacing the one-hot LightGBM in file 15’s blend	+0.00013
Rate model added on top of the blend (best weight)	+0.00009
I also tried a differently weighted loss, with defaulters weighted 4 times more, as a blend member. The first run had a bug (it stopped training after 6 rounds). The corrected run scored 0.76188 alone and added nothing to the blend (−0.00000 to −0.00020). ChatGPT’s own latest attempt (~0.7679) also did not beat 0.76794, so we agree.

What to do

Keep GCI_submission_final.csv (0.76794) as your last upload. Nothing I’ve tested beats it.
Ask the course organizers on Slack whether the zip can be submitted if you are outside the top 15. Based on the pattern in this thread, I don’t think any honest method I’ve found will close a +0.0087 gap.
Keep your three files together (CSV, .py and README), in case the rule changes.
I’m not sending any new scripts.

0.76811

I made a new CSV and a single script, but I expect only a small gain, about +0.0003 to +0.0004, which is at the edge of leaderboard noise. Your 0.76811 puts you at rank 66, and I can’t honestly promise a top-15 finish.

What I tested from ChatGPT’s file

I read the transcript and re-tested its two new ideas with held-out folds and no label leakage.

EXT_SOURCE divisions plus exact-rate features: not real.
The model scores 0.76165 vs 0.76288 without the divisions, and adds only +0.00001 to the blend.
Its “+0.003 to +0.004” holdout gains came from a weak baseline (one test used only 300 trees).
If your 0.76811 file contained this model, the gain was noise or came from something else in the file.
I believe the 0.76811 file is the 90/10 blend at the end of the transcript; tell me if it was the residual file instead.
Loan-template residuals: real.
The idea takes an exact (credit, annuity) loan template and measures the extra default risk of a customer state inside it, after removing the template’s own risk. It is the same idea as the fingerprint.
I screened 48 template × state combinations. Picking the best on four folds and applying it to the fifth gained +0.00030 on average, positive in 5 of 5 folds.
The average of the top six gains +0.00042 at a 5% weight, also positive in 5 of 5 folds.
They overlap, so stacking more adds nothing.
The files

GCI_submission_residual.csv: 95% of your champion file plus 5% of the averaged residual score. It has 61,500 rows, the exact columns, IDs in sample order, and no missing values. It correlates 0.99898 with the champion.
GCI_residual_submission.py: one file that produces that CSV from train.csv, test.csv and your champion CSV. I ran it and compared it with an independent recomputation, and the maximum difference was 1e-16.
README_FINAL.md: updated with a section on this step.
The leaderboard

Rank 15 is now 0.77727 and rank 10 is 0.77802, about +0.009 above 0.76811. Our held-out evidence supports about +0.0004 more, and the top scores are far beyond what these 32 columns support. I will not help with external data or labels. Upload the new CSV as your next submission, and keep your best-scoring file as your last upload.

a very small improvement good job a better score but not a good now we need a lot to move up in the leader board but now we got an idea that feature engineering is not enough because after finind that formula we did a lot of test without formula we might not have reached this score we need a lot of great ways like that too optimise the feature engineering a lot better and advanced!

Another new best: 0.76815. I’ll be straight about the attribution, because it changes what we do next. The exact-rate formula itself added nothing, since it is a re-labelling of a ratio we already had. The gain came from a different mechanism: memorising exact loan templates and measuring the extra risk of customer states inside each one. That is information the tree models can’t learn on their own, because it needs exact-value memory. Better feature engineering means finding more of that kind of signal, and I have one candidate that goes a level deeper.

The current residual measures extra risk relative to the template’s own average. The deeper version measures what the model itself gets wrong inside each template, using leakage-safe nested predictions. That is a real test of information the features and models miss.

The "22% repeat-applicant coverage" is a bug

The cause: pensioners have no employment date, so their key is missing, and the matching code counts missing = missing as a match. The 11,832 "matched" test rows are pensioners matching other pensioners; the "matched keys" in the output are literally nan.
The correct number: the employment-based key links 1,267 test rows (2.1%). Its precision is lower than the strict key's: when the partner defaulted, 27% default against 42% for the strict key.
The "90% pure" and "54–64%" claims: about 85% purity is what random pairs give at an 8% default rate. The 54–64% figure counts each row's own label.
With the keys computed correctly: together they add 1,997 test rows (3.25%) beyond what v16 covers, about 7% in total.
Does it help? I tested adding them against the true baseline: −0.00068, +0.00013, −0.00024, +0.00066, +0.00013, a mean of 0.0000. Rejected.
Other claims in that analysis, such as "target OOF 0.7785 maps to leaderboard 0.78616", have no supporting evidence.
What to do with the results

Blending dilution is confirmed: weaker standalone models lower a stronger champion. That matches our own ledger.
Your best submission is v22 (0.78239). It is a 55/45 blend of v17 and v18, and in effect ties our champion.

# 4. The strongest discovery so far: amortization-derived rate shape

The most successful feature-engineering direction so far has been a mathematical repayment-geometry signal.

The core financial relationship is:

ANNUITY / CREDIT = r(1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1)

where:
- r is an implied periodic interest rate,
- n is the assumed loan duration in months,
- ANNUITY is the regular payment,
- CREDIT is the loan amount.

We invert the annuity relationship for several possible durations, including 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 84, and 120 months, then derive shape features from the implied-rate curve.

Derived features have included:
- Curve slopes.
- Curvature at different durations.
- rate_range.
- rate_std.
- Short-versus-long duration differences.
- Mid-curve bends.
- Related curve-geometry descriptors.

A small residual correction based on these features improved the public score across several iterations:

0.76815 → 0.76835 → 0.76854 → 0.76858.

This is our most credible successful research path.

However, do not automatically conclude that every additional rate-curve feature will help. More flexible corrections have sometimes damaged the score.

---

# 5. RD3 fingerprint correction

Another investigated idea was an RD3 fingerprint involving:

rate_range × rate_std × r24

The original idea binned these features into a 12 × 12 × 5 grid and applied shrinkage with alpha 100.

The initial research report described a promising archival validation, with 75/75 positive results and a mean gain of roughly +0.000603 at a 5% correction weight. But those results came from an older model/residual setup and do not establish transfer to the exact current champion.

Important limitation: the exact out-of-fold residuals for the 0.76858 champion are not available in the currently documented artifacts. Therefore, the RD3 correction was transferred to the champion without a complete champion-aligned residual validation.

Public evidence showed:
- Forward corrections at stronger weights were generally worse.
- Smaller forward corrections approached the champion but remained below it.
- Some reverse corrections were close to the champion or slightly above it at displayed precision.

Possible explanation:
The RD3 fingerprint may contain useful information, but its learned residual direction or scale may not transfer correctly to the champion. Reversing the sign can sometimes improve ranking if the original residual effect was overestimated, inverted, or model-dependent.

This is a hypothesis, not proof. We need the exact formulas, residual source, gates, and candidate-generation scripts before making a strong causal claim.

Useful directory:

/mnt/data/rate_shape_champion_candidate/

Relevant files include:
- GCI_RD3_transfer_w01_gate93.csv
- GCI_RD3_transfer_w02_gate93.csv

# 6. CatBoost model diversity experiment

A separate experiment tested whether CatBoost could add complementary ranking information to a LightGBM baseline.

Target-free engineered features included:
- credit_annuity = AMT_CREDIT / AMT_ANNUITY
- annuity_income = AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_INCOME_TOTAL
- credit_income = AMT_CREDIT / AMT_INCOME_TOTAL
- AGE_YEARS = -DAYS_BIRTH / 365.25
- External-source score mean, standard deviation, and range.

The model experiment compared:
- LightGBM with 300 boosting rounds, learning rate 0.035, 31 leaves, minimum child samples 200, subsampling 0.85, column sampling 0.80, L2 regularization 25, alpha 0.5, and max bin 127.
- CatBoost with 120 iterations, depth 5, learning rate 0.06, and L2 regularization 8.

Two independent two-fold partitions were tested.

### Partition seed 271828

LightGBM overall OOF AUC: 0.75255654.
CatBoost overall OOF AUC: 0.74648130.
Rank/probability blends against the LightGBM OOF predictions showed a positive trend in AUC on that split, reaching approximately +0.001221 at a 30% CatBoost blend.
### Partition seed 314159

LightGBM overall OOF AUC: 0.75211439.
CatBoost overall OOF AUC: 0.74642932.
Blend gains also increased with CatBoost weight against that LightGBM baseline, reaching approximately +0.001356 at 30%.
These results show that CatBoost may contribute complementary ranking information to this particular LightGBM baseline despite having lower standalone AUC.

But they do NOT prove improvement against the rate-shape champion. The validation used only two-fold discovery experiments and did not use the exact champion's OOF errors.

Full-data CatBoost model and experimental submission:

/mnt/data/gci_research_round3/run_gci_catboost_diversity.py

Round 3 candidate:

/mnt/data/gci_research_round3/GCI_experimental_champion_catboost_rankblend_5pct.csv

Round 4 files:

/mnt/data/gci_research_round4/

The 5% candidate scored 0.76851, lower than the champion. The 1% candidate tied at 0.76858 at displayed precision. The 3% and 4% candidates scored 0.76857 and 0.76856.

Possible explanation:
CatBoost has some complementary information, but the standalone model is weaker and its errors may overlap substantially with the champion's errors. Even a genuinely diverse model can hurt AUC if it perturbs too many correct pairwise orderings.

A small blend weight limits damage but also limits potential improvement. More model diversity is needed, preferably supported by champion-aligned OOF comparisons.

# 7. Other experiments that did not transfer well

Previously investigated directions include:
- Adaptive rate-shape corrections.
- Affordability-curve residual correction.
- Financial-template corrections.
- External-score interaction corrections.
- Generic model changes.
- Stacking.
- K-nearest-neighbor-derived corrections.
- Risk-stratified residual corrections.
- Peer-group residual corrections.

Some approaches showed promising local results but performed worse on the public leaderboard.

Examples:
- Adaptive top-30% rate-shape correction, weight 8%, scored 0.76820.
- Affordability-curve residual correction, weight 8%, scored 0.76793.
- CA × EXT1 correction at 3% scored 0.76837.
- RD3 RangeStd at 15% scored 0.76835.

Do not repeat these exact methods without a materially improved formulation and a clear reason for expecting better transfer.

### Why locally promising methods can fail publicly

Potential explanations include:

**Validation mismatch:** The local split or model differs from the leaderboard-generating process.
**Champion mismatch:** A residual correction trained for one model may not correct another model's errors.
**Overfitting:** A high-dimensional residual grid can fit noise in the validation data.
**Wrong correction sign or scale:** The residual direction may not transfer to test data.
**Pairwise ranking damage:** A correction may improve predicted probabilities or a subgroup metric while reversing many correct positive-negative pairs, hurting ROC-AUC.
**Distribution differences:** A small overall train/test shift can still coexist with subgroup-specific shifts.
**Small gains and leaderboard noise:** Differences of 0.00001–0.00007 may not represent a robust improvement, especially when reported scores are rounded.
These are candidate explanations. Do not assert one is the proven cause unless supporting diagnostics exist.

---

# 8. Train/test shift investigation

An adversarial validation experiment previously found an approximate ROC-AUC around 0.51756, indicating only a small overall train/test difference.

A later run initially produced an apparent 0.656, but this was an in-sample overfit diagnostic. The proper three-fold estimate was around 0.51565.

Therefore:
- Do not claim there is a major train/test shift.
- Do not claim that distribution shift explains all failed candidates.
- Consider subgroup-specific shift analysis only if supported by data.
- Use cross-validation or out-of-fold predictions, not in-sample scores.

---

# 11. Research plan: what to do next

I want more ambitious research than changing blend weights.

## Priority A — Recover the strongest model's OOF artifacts

Search the available folders for:
- Champion-generating scripts.
- Original train/validation split definitions.
- Out-of-fold predictions.
- Fold-level predictions and labels.
- Residuals or error analysis.
- Feature definitions and formulas.
- Saved models and model parameters.

If the exact champion OOF predictions are missing, recreate the closest feasible model and generate stratified five-fold OOF predictions using fixed, documented splits.

Be honest: a recreated model is not automatically identical to the champion.

## Priority B — Champion-aligned error analysis

For a trustworthy OOF model:
- Calculate ROC-AUC on each fold.
- Examine positive-negative pairwise ranking errors.
- Analyze residuals or ranking failures by credit amount, annuity, income, age, external-source scores, implied-rate geometry, categorical groups, missingness, and relevant interactions.
- Use train-only features.
- Search for repeatable patterns across folds, not just one lucky split.

Do not learn a correction from the validation fold's labels and then evaluate it on those same rows.

## Priority C — New mathematically motivated repayment features

Investigate whether repayment geometry adds information beyond ordinary ratios.

Potential hypotheses to test:
- Implied rate estimates across several possible loan durations.
- Curve smoothness and curvature.
- Consistency of credit, annuity, goods price, and income.
- Payment burden relative to income.
- Duration ambiguity and rate-curve shape.
- Interactions between repayment geometry and external credit scores.
- Nonlinear behavior by missingness or employment categories.

These are research directions, not established discoveries. Derive features from available columns, document formulas, handle zero denominators and missing values, and validate them carefully.

## Priority D — Test model diversity correctly

Compare LightGBM, CatBoost, and other justified models using the same folds.

Measure:
- Standalone OOF ROC-AUC.
- Pairwise/rank correlation between predictions.
- Blend AUC over a small predefined weight grid.
- Fold-by-fold gains.
- Stability across random seeds.
- Whether a blend improves on the strongest baseline, not merely on a weaker model.

Do not assume a lower-AUC model is useful simply because it is different.

## Priority E — Robust evaluation

Use stratified five-fold cross-validation where practical, with a fixed random seed and no leakage. If the computational budget is limited, begin with a smaller discovery run, then verify the strongest candidates on independent splits.

Report:
- Exact metrics.
- Fold-level results.
- Mean and variability across folds.
- Baseline comparison.
- Whether feature selection or correction tuning used the same validation results.
- Whether the result is discovery evidence or independent confirmation.

Do not confuse probability calibration with ranking improvement. ROC-AUC is based on ordering.

## Priority F — Submission discipline

Protect the 0.76858 champion.
Generate separate, clearly named candidates.
Verify ID order, row count, columns, uniqueness, and finite values.
Submit only candidates with a reason to expect improvement.
Record exact public scores immediately.
Stop unproductive sweeps when results repeatedly plateau.
Keep an experiment ledger containing formulas, parameters, local results, public scores, and decisions.
---

# 12. Required response style

Work like a rigorous ML research engineer and a collaborative partner.

Before coding:
1. Inspect the existing artifacts.
2. Identify what is already known.
3. State the specific hypothesis.
4. Explain why it could improve ROC-AUC.
5. Define the evaluation that could disprove the hypothesis.

After coding:
1. Provide the actual script and candidate files.
2. Report real validation outputs.
3. Explain what improved and what did not.
4. Clearly separate observed facts from possible explanations.
5. Preserve the champion.
6. Recommend the next step based on evidence.

Do not:
- Fabricate experiment results.
- Promise 0.77000.
- Present a rounded 0.7686 as a proven gain.
- Repeat failed experiments without changing the underlying hypothesis.
- Use in-sample AUC as evidence of generalization.
- Claim that a candidate is better simply because it uses more features or a more complex model.
- Hide negative results.

Determine whether the exact champion OOF predictions and residuals exist. If they do, use them. If they do not, explain what is missing and design the closest scientifically valid alternative.

Then run the next experiment that has the strongest evidence-based chance of adding useful ranking information beyond the current champion.

---

# 4. The strongest discovery so far: amortization-derived rate shape

The most successful feature-engineering direction so far has been a mathematical repayment-geometry signal.

The core financial relationship is:

`ANNUITY / CREDIT = r(1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1)`

where:
- `r` is an implied periodic interest rate,
- `n` is the assumed loan duration in months,
- `ANNUITY` is the regular payment,
- `CREDIT` is the loan amount.

We invert the annuity relationship for several possible durations, including 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 84, and 120 months, then derive shape features from the implied-rate curve.

Derived features have included:
- Curve slopes.
- Curvature at different durations.
- `rate_range`.
- `rate_std`.
- Short-versus-long duration differences.
- Mid-curve bends.
- Related curve-geometry descriptors.

A small residual correction based on these features improved the public score across several iterations:

0.76815 → 0.76835 → 0.76854 → 0.76858.

This is our most credible successful research path.

However, do not automatically conclude that every additional rate-curve feature will help. More flexible corrections have sometimes damaged the score.

---

# 5. RD3 fingerprint correction

Another investigated idea was an RD3 fingerprint involving:

`rate_range × rate_std × r24`

The original idea binned these features into a 12 × 12 × 5 grid and applied shrinkage with alpha 100.

The initial research report described a promising archival validation, with 75/75 positive results and a mean gain of roughly +0.000603 at a 5% correction weight. But those results came from an older model/residual setup and do not establish transfer to the exact current champion.

Important limitation: the exact out-of-fold residuals for the 0.76858 champion are not available in the currently documented artifacts. Therefore, the RD3 correction was transferred to the champion without a complete champion-aligned residual validation.

Public evidence showed:
- Forward corrections at stronger weights were generally worse.
- Smaller forward corrections approached the champion but remained below it.
- Some reverse corrections were close to the champion or slightly above it at displayed precision.

Possible explanation:
The RD3 fingerprint may contain useful information, but its learned residual direction or scale may not transfer correctly to the champion. Reversing the sign can sometimes improve ranking if the original residual effect was overestimated, inverted, or model-dependent.

This is a hypothesis, not proof. We need the exact formulas, residual source, gates, and candidate-generation scripts before making a strong causal claim.

Useful directory:

`/mnt/data/rate_shape_champion_candidate/`

Relevant files include:
- `GCI_RD3_transfer_w01_gate93.csv`
- `GCI_RD3_transfer_w02_gate93.csv`

# 6. CatBoost model diversity experiment

A separate experiment tested whether CatBoost could add complementary ranking information to a LightGBM baseline.

Target-free engineered features included:
- `credit_annuity = AMT_CREDIT / AMT_ANNUITY`
- `annuity_income = AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_INCOME_TOTAL`
- `credit_income = AMT_CREDIT / AMT_INCOME_TOTAL`
- `AGE_YEARS = -DAYS_BIRTH / 365.25`
- External-source score mean, standard deviation, and range.

The model experiment compared:
- LightGBM with 300 boosting rounds, learning rate 0.035, 31 leaves, minimum child samples 200, subsampling 0.85, column sampling 0.80, L2 regularization 25, alpha 0.5, and max bin 127.
- CatBoost with 120 iterations, depth 5, learning rate 0.06, and L2 regularization 8.

Two independent two-fold partitions were tested.

### Partition seed 271828

- LightGBM overall OOF AUC: 0.75255654.
- CatBoost overall OOF AUC: 0.74648130.
- Rank/probability blends against the LightGBM OOF predictions showed a positive trend in AUC on that split, reaching approximately +0.001221 at a 30% CatBoost blend.

### Partition seed 314159

- LightGBM overall OOF AUC: 0.75211439.
- CatBoost overall OOF AUC: 0.74642932.
- Blend gains also increased with CatBoost weight against that LightGBM baseline, reaching approximately +0.001356 at 30%.

These results show that CatBoost may contribute complementary ranking information to this particular LightGBM baseline despite having lower standalone AUC.

But they do NOT prove improvement against the rate-shape champion. The validation used only two-fold discovery experiments and did not use the exact champion's OOF errors.

Full-data CatBoost model and experimental submission:

`/mnt/data/gci_research_round3/run_gci_catboost_diversity.py`

Round 3 candidate:

`/mnt/data/gci_research_round3/GCI_experimental_champion_catboost_rankblend_5pct.csv`

Round 4 files:

`/mnt/data/gci_research_round4/`

The 5% candidate scored 0.76851, lower than the champion. The 1% candidate tied at 0.76858 at displayed precision. The 3% and 4% candidates scored 0.76857 and 0.76856.

Possible explanation:
CatBoost has some complementary information, but the standalone model is weaker and its errors may overlap substantially with the champion's errors. Even a genuinely diverse model can hurt AUC if it perturbs too many correct pairwise orderings.

A small blend weight limits damage but also limits potential improvement. More model diversity is needed, preferably supported by champion-aligned OOF comparisons.

---

# 7. Other experiments that did not transfer well

Previously investigated directions include:
- Adaptive rate-shape corrections.
- Affordability-curve residual correction.
- Financial-template corrections.
- External-score interaction corrections.
- Generic model changes.
- Stacking.
- K-nearest-neighbor-derived corrections.
- Risk-stratified residual corrections.
- Peer-group residual corrections.

Some approaches showed promising local results but performed worse on the public leaderboard.

Examples:
- Adaptive top-30% rate-shape correction, weight 8%, scored 0.76820.
- Affordability-curve residual correction, weight 8%, scored 0.76793.
- CA × EXT1 correction at 3% scored 0.76837.
- RD3 RangeStd at 15% scored 0.76835.

Do not repeat these exact methods without a materially improved formulation and a clear reason for expecting better transfer.

### Why locally promising methods can fail publicly

Potential explanations include:

1. **Validation mismatch:** The local split or model differs from the leaderboard-generating process.
2. **Champion mismatch:** A residual correction trained for one model may not correct another model's errors.
3. **Overfitting:** A high-dimensional residual grid can fit noise in the validation data.
4. **Wrong correction sign or scale:** The residual direction may not transfer to test data.
5. **Pairwise ranking damage:** A correction may improve predicted probabilities or a subgroup metric while reversing many correct positive-negative pairs, hurting ROC-AUC.
6. **Distribution differences:** A small overall train/test shift can still coexist with subgroup-specific shifts.
7. **Small gains and leaderboard noise:** Differences of 0.00001–0.00007 may not represent a robust improvement, especially when reported scores are rounded.

These are candidate explanations. Do not assert one is the proven cause unless supporting diagnostics exist.

---

# 8. Train/test shift investigation

An adversarial validation experiment previously found an approximate ROC-AUC around 0.51756, indicating only a small overall train/test difference.

A later run initially produced an apparent 0.656, but this was an in-sample overfit diagnostic. The proper three-fold estimate was around 0.51565.

Therefore:
- Do not claim there is a major train/test shift.
- Do not claim that distribution shift explains all failed candidates.
- Consider subgroup-specific shift analysis only if supported by data.
- Use cross-validation or out-of-fold predictions, not in-sample scores.

---

# 11. Research plan: what to do next

I want more ambitious research than changing blend weights.

## Priority A — Recover the strongest model's OOF artifacts

Search the available folders for:
- Champion-generating scripts.
- Original train/validation split definitions.
- Out-of-fold predictions.
- Fold-level predictions and labels.
- Residuals or error analysis.
- Feature definitions and formulas.
- Saved models and model parameters.

If the exact champion OOF predictions are missing, recreate the closest feasible model and generate stratified five-fold OOF predictions using fixed, documented splits.

Be honest: a recreated model is not automatically identical to the champion.

## Priority B — Champion-aligned error analysis

For a trustworthy OOF model:
- Calculate ROC-AUC on each fold.
- Examine positive-negative pairwise ranking errors.
- Analyze residuals or ranking failures by credit amount, annuity, income, age, external-source scores, implied-rate geometry, categorical groups, missingness, and relevant interactions.
- Use train-only features.
- Search for repeatable patterns across folds, not just one lucky split.

Do not learn a correction from the validation fold's labels and then evaluate it on those same rows.

## Priority C — New mathematically motivated repayment features

Investigate whether repayment geometry adds information beyond ordinary ratios.

Potential hypotheses to test:
- Implied rate estimates across several possible loan durations.
- Curve smoothness and curvature.
- Consistency of credit, annuity, goods price, and income.
- Payment burden relative to income.
- Duration ambiguity and rate-curve shape.
- Interactions between repayment geometry and external credit scores.
- Nonlinear behavior by missingness or employment categories.

These are research directions, not established discoveries. Derive features from available columns, document formulas, handle zero denominators and missing values, and validate them carefully.

## Priority D — Test model diversity correctly

Compare LightGBM, CatBoost, and other justified models using the same folds.

Measure:
- Standalone OOF ROC-AUC.
- Pairwise/rank correlation between predictions.
- Blend AUC over a small predefined weight grid.
- Fold-by-fold gains.
- Stability across random seeds.
- Whether a blend improves on the strongest baseline, not merely on a weaker model.

Do not assume a lower-AUC model is useful simply because it is different.

## Priority E — Robust evaluation

Use stratified five-fold cross-validation where practical, with a fixed random seed and no leakage. If the computational budget is limited, begin with a smaller discovery run, then verify the strongest candidates on independent splits.

Report:
- Exact metrics.
- Fold-level results.
- Mean and variability across folds.
- Baseline comparison.
- Whether feature selection or correction tuning used the same validation results.
- Whether the result is discovery evidence or independent confirmation.

Do not confuse probability calibration with ranking improvement. ROC-AUC is based on ordering.

## Priority F — Submission discipline

- Protect the 0.76858 champion.
- Generate separate, clearly named candidates.
- Verify ID order, row count, columns, uniqueness, and finite values.
- Submit only candidates with a reason to expect improvement.
- Record exact public scores immediately.
- Stop unproductive sweeps when results repeatedly plateau.
- Keep an experiment ledger containing formulas, parameters, local results, public scores, and decisions.

---

# 12. Required response style

Work like a rigorous ML research engineer and a collaborative partner.

Before coding:
1. Inspect the existing artifacts.
2. Identify what is already known.
3. State the specific hypothesis.
4. Explain why it could improve ROC-AUC.
5. Define the evaluation that could disprove the hypothesis.

After coding:
1. Provide the actual script and candidate files.
2. Report real validation outputs.
3. Explain what improved and what did not.
4. Clearly separate observed facts from possible explanations.
5. Preserve the champion.
6. Recommend the next step based on evidence.

Do not:
- Fabricate experiment results.
- Promise 0.77000.
- Present a rounded 0.7686 as a proven gain.
- Repeat failed experiments without changing the underlying hypothesis.
- Use in-sample AUC as evidence of generalization.
- Claim that a candidate is better simply because it uses more features or a more complex model.
- Hide negative results.

Begin by inspecting the existing research artifacts, especially `systematic_champion_meta`, the five-fold experiment folders, and the scripts or predictions that created the rate-shape champion.

Determine whether the exact champion OOF predictions and residuals exist. If they do, use them. If they do not, explain what is missing and design the closest scientifically valid alternative.

Then run the next experiment that has the strongest evidence-based chance of adding useful ranking information beyond the current champion.

# GCI World 2026 - Research Notes (everything we learned)

Last updated after the q-fingerprint submission scored **0.77283** on the public leaderboard (previous best 0.76815).
This file is written so that you (or a new chat/assistant) can continue the competition without re-doing work. Facts are separated from guesses; every number says whether it is a
held-out (leak-free) estimate or a public-leaderboard score.

## 0. TL;DR
1. **The big discovery:** every "rate-curve shape" feature (rate_std, rate_range, r24, curvatures...) is a function of ONE number, `q = AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_CREDIT`.
   The real signal is default risk as a function of `q` at fine resolution (about 800-1,500 distinct values = lender loan products). Trees cannot resolve it with default binning.
   Giving LightGBM smoothed, leak-safe target encodings of `q` (rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals) lifted a single model from 0.76265 to 0.76713 held-out, and the public score from 0.76815 to **0.77283**.
2. The held-out method (below) has predicted every public move to within about 0.0003 - trust it, not local numbers from weak models or the rounded public board.
3. Everything that is a re-expression of columns the trees already see (ratios, logs, EXT divisions, harmonic means, exact rates) is worth ~0. Gains come from information trees cannot learn: fine-resolution product identity (q), exact loan templates, more rows/seeds, diverse models.
4. 0.785 would be +0.012 over 0.77283 (about 2.6 times what the q discovery added). Nothing found so far supports promising it; the best chance is hunting for a second "hidden structure" like q (section 9).
5. Rules: only the supplied train/test/sample files. No external data/labels, no hand-labeling, no test labels. Final ranking uses the private leaderboard and your LAST submitted file. Honors/Outstanding needs code that reproduces the CSV, and AI-assisted code must be disclosed.

## 1. Public score ledger (what the board said)
Start: your XGBoost 0.76308; earlier champion 0.76370.

## 2. Competition facts and rules (from the README)
- Metric ROC-AUC. Train 171,202 rows (34 columns incl. TARGET), test 61,500 rows (33 columns, no TARGET - `test.csv` has NO labels), submission `SK_ID_CURR,TARGET`, exactly 61,500 rows, IDs in sample order.
- No external data at any stage; no hand-labeling (rule-based edits of individual predictions from EDA); only supplied data.
- Final ranking: private leaderboard (full test set) using your last submitted file. Public board = interim subset. Ends Nov 20, 01:00 UTC; results Dec 17.
- Honors/Outstanding Student: submit code that reproduces your CSV; top-ranking participants must disclose code and cite sources (uncited code can lead to a revoked prize).
- Submitting a research table instead of a prediction file returns -999 (happened 3 times in the ChatGPT chain).

## 3. Data facts
- 32 predictors: 24 numeric + 8 categorical (NAME_CONTRACT_TYPE, CODE_GENDER, NAME_INCOME_TYPE, NAME_EDUCATION_TYPE, NAME_FAMILY_STATUS, NAME_HOUSING_TYPE, OCCUPATION_TYPE, ORGANIZATION_TYPE). Default rate 8.07%.
- SK_ID_CURR was re-indexed: train 0-171,201, test 171,202-232,701 (no leak in the ID; row order carries no signal, AUC 0.503). No duplicate rows within or across train/test.
- DAYS_EMPLOYED = 365243 is a sentinel (18% of train rows; default 5.4% vs 8.7%). Missingness: EXT_SOURCE_1 69.5%, OWN_CAR_AGE 66%, EXT_SOURCE_3 31.9%; missingness itself carries signal.
- No major train/test shift (PSI < 0.005 on top features; adversarial AUC about 0.516). EXT_SOURCE_1/2/3 are the strongest raw features (univariate AUC 0.66-0.68).
- Only 0.3% of rows share person-level fields (birth, registration, ID dates); the same-person link is real (0.88 AUC inside groups) but covers too few rows to matter (+0.0001).

## 4. The q-fingerprint discovery (details)
**Why it works.** For an amortizing loan, ANNUITY/CREDIT = r(1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1). For any fixed term n, the implied rate is a strictly increasing function of q = ANNUITY/CREDIT, so all rate/curvature/dispersion features computed from q are functions of q alone
(R^2 of each shape feature against a 400-bin function of q: 0.9993-1.0000). They add nothing to a tree that already has q (the exact-rate features alone gave +0.0002 when models were trained properly).
What matters is that **risk varies sharply across specific q values**: q rounded to 4 decimals has about 814 distinct values and every row shares its value with at least 5 others - lender products (rate x term combinations).
LightGBM's default 255 bins and regularization cannot resolve this; a smoothed lookup of default rate by q scores 0.675 AUC alone (earlier exact-value fingerprints ~0.62).
**How it is built (leak-safe).** For each key (q rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals): TE = (sum_y + m0 * prior) / (count + m0) with m0 = 100, 30, 30, plus the count. Training rows use inner 5-fold out-of-fold encodings (seed 7);
validation/test rows use all rows of the training portion only. Outer folds: StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle, seed 42).
**Evidence (all held-out, same folds).**
- As a blend member only: +0.00125 at 10% weight (+0.00111 at 7%, +0.00101 at 15%, -0.0034 at 30%); choosing the best member on 4 folds and scoring the 5th: +0.00118, 5/5 folds positive.
- As FEATURES inside LightGBM (the strong version): 0.76713 vs 0.76265 without (+0.00448). Features beat the blend by 4x.
- Three models: qf_onehot 0.76713, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678, qf_mono 0.76746; average 0.76843 (previous best recipe 0.76458); recipe + average at W=0.7: 0.76861 (+0.00403); W chosen on 4 folds scored on the 5th: +0.00408, 5/5 positive; optimum flat for W in 0.7-1.0.
- Resolution matters: 182 distinct values +0.00102 (blend test); 814 values +0.00125; 1,493 values +0.00127. Shrinkage does not (m0 10-60 all about +0.0012).
- Other keys as extra features gave nothing (first 4 of 5 folds, change vs q-only: +0.00009, +0.00008, -0.00014, -0.00052): goods/credit (3 and 4 decimals), credit/income, annuity/income, q x goods ratio, q x contract type. q is special.
- As blend members, only q x contract type added anything among 12 customer states; the others hurt.
**Prediction vs reality.** Held-out predicted +0.0040; public gave +0.0047 (0.76815 -> 0.77283).
**Why the rate-shape/RD3 chain only moved +0.0001-0.0002 per step:** it was a coarse, 1-D, weak-anchored approximation of this signal, applied as a tiny blend.

## 5. Validation method (use this, and its traps)
- Held-out = every training row predicted by a model that never saw it (5-fold, seed 42). Fixed boosting rounds chosen WITHOUT the held-out labels (early stopping on the held-out fold is optimistic; it inflated an early pseudo-label "gain" to +0.0008 when the clean result was -0.0014).
- Target-derived features must be nested (inner out-of-fold for training rows). Never use in-sample predictions to build residuals.
- A correction fitted against a WEAK anchor model does not transfer: RD3 (anchor weaker than the champion), model-residual templates (fast base 0.759), EXT-division/rate models with under-trained baselines all showed "+0.003" locally and ~0 or negative publicly/against a strong base.
- A pre-registered test: choose weights/members on 4 folds, score on the 5th, repeat; need 5/5 positive. Public noise (0.0001-0.0005) is larger than most single ideas - decide with held-out, confirm with ONE public submission.
- test.csv has no labels; only the leaderboard scores test predictions. Do not loop on the public board.

Final 67-feature set = clean + fin + age + bureau.

**More rows:** learning curve 38.5k/77k/115.5k/154k rows -> 0.75104/0.75795/0.76092/0.76387 (about +0.003 per 38k rows). 5-fold -> 10-fold -> full-data refit each helped. **More seeds:** 1 seed 0.7616 -> 3 0.7624 -> 5 0.7627 -> 8 0.7633.

**Diversity blends:** all-families LGB (130 features: EXT aggregates, count encodings, group differences) is weak alone but blends well (+0.0004 at 20%); XGB slightly hurts the blend; high-capacity LGB, one-hot XGB, native categoricals, top-40 features: nothing.
**Loan-template fingerprints/residuals:** exact (income, annuity, credit, goods) hierarchy fingerprint alone 0.62148, blend +0.00045 at 5%; template-own-risk residual (exact credit/annuity [CA] and with goods [CAG] x contract/housing/employment sentinel): 48 combos screened, best single +0.00036, average of top 6 +0.00042 at 5% (5/5 folds; public +0.0002); 189-candidate extended screen and model-residual versions: 0.
**Rejected after clean tests:** pseudo-labelling (-0.0014); exact-rate features (+0.00023); EXT divisions + rate (0.76165 vs 0.76288); harmonic EXT + loan-to-value (-0.00024 alone, blend +0.0001); max_bin=511 (+0.0002 alone); weighted loss (0); KNN target rate (-0.002); target encoding of categoricals; MLP/NN (sklearn, 0.736-0.745 alone, blend <= 0); same-person feature (+0.0001); CatBoost (too slow here; ChatGPT's 1-5% blends were 0 to -0.00007 publicly).
**Research suggesting NNs:** RealMLP/TabM with tuning+ensembling can match GBDTs and blend well on 1K-500K rows (Holzmueller et al., NeurIPS 2024 https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04491; TabArena https://alphaxiv.org/abs/2506.16791). Not tested here (no torch/GPU): see notebook section 4.
**Leaderboard context (table you shared before the q discovery):** rank 15 was 0.77727, rank 10 0.77802, rank 1 0.79101; you were around rank 66-70 at 0.768. Use the current board; scores above 0.777 on these 32 columns are far beyond what any held-out test of ours supported before q.

## 7. Final architecture (the file that scored 0.77283)
final = 0.30 x GCI_submission_residual.csv + 0.70 x mean rank of three q-feature LightGBM models.
- GCI_submission_residual.csv = 0.95 x file 15 + 0.05 x mean rank of six template-residual scores [CAGxCONTRACT, CAxCONTRACT, CAGxHOUSING, CAxHOUSING, CAxDE_SENT, CAGxDE_SENT]; file 15 = weights 12% monotone LGB, 12% shallow LGB, 36% one-hot LGB, 16% all-families LGB, 20% one-hot all-families LGB, 4% exact-value fingerprint.
- Each q-model member = 50% average of 5 fold-models + 50% average of 3 full-data seeds (ranks). Parameters and rounds are in `GCI_q_fingerprint_colab.ipynb` (and `GCI_qfingerprint_submission.py`).
- Verified reproducible: the script's full-data predictions for qf_allfam_oh and qf_mono equal the saved ones exactly; the full pipeline was not re-run end-to-end in one go.

## 9. Research plan beyond 0.77283 (ranked; every item untested unless stated)
Use the notebook's `test_extra_key` (5-fold, leak-free, ~10 min) to test candidates; only a gain above ~+0.0003 with the same folds is worth a public submission.
1. **Hunt for a second "hidden structure" like q.** q worked because lenders generate loan terms by formula. Look for other values generated by formulas/catalogs: exact AMT_CREDIT, AMT_GOODS_PRICE, AMT_ANNUITY, AMT_INCOME_TOTAL values as keys (as FEATURES, not blends); round-number patterns (value mod 1,000 / 5,000 / 9,000, last digits, multiples of 4.5); annuity per month vs goods price; DAYS_BIRTH as a key.
2. **Re-do the whole ensemble with q-features inside every member** (shallow LGB, extra seeds, XGBoost, CatBoost) and re-run the 25-step ensemble search; then re-test the residual-template and fingerprint blends against this stronger base (they were tuned against a weaker one and may now overlap).
3. **Tune the q-models now that they carry more information:** more rounds/leaves, lower learning rate, 10-fold, 8+ seeds, full-data refits (the seed and learning curves say +0.001 or more).
4. **Pairwise error analysis against the NEW champion** with strict out-of-fold predictions (notebook gives OOF): where does the champion rank positives below negatives? Group by q bin, contract type, EXT missingness, product groups.
5. **Higher-resolution q keys** (round 6 decimals; hierarchical backoff) and q-conditioned encodings as features for sub-populations (contract type was the only helpful state as a blend; others hurt).
6. **NN diversity (RealMLP/TabM) on Colab GPU with the q-features** - earlier NNs were weak (0.745) and blend-negative; low expectation, listed for completeness.
7. Do NOT repeat: harmonic EXT, exact-rate/EXT-division features, pseudo-labelling, adaptive gating, KNN residuals, global meta-models, weighted loss, extra ratio keys (goods/credit, credit/income, annuity/income).
**Honest expectation:** 0.785 needs about +0.012. The q discovery was worth +0.0047 and came from asking "what structure generates these numbers?". Another discovery of similar size is possible but not guaranteed; do not plan around it.

## 12. Update: product-key decode (your 0.77715 notebook), verification, and what is left
**Scores added to the ledger:** the Colab notebook output (3 q-feature LightGBM models + our blend) **0.77321**; the product-key notebook blend **0.77715** (+0.0039). Held-out numbers in the product-key notebook as reported by you
(not recomputed by me except the check below): qf_onehot 0.76713 -> 0.76992, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678 -> 0.77070, qf_mono 0.76746 -> 0.77035, 3-member average 0.76843 -> 0.77158.
**The decode (label-free):** annuity = Q0 x credit + F, where F is a fixed monthly fee (about 129.5; a plateau of 125.5-130.5 scores the same) on about 75.1% of cash loans. A row is flagged as fee-bearing when (annuity - 129.5)/credit has more rows sharing its 5-decimal value than annuity/credit does.
Q0 = fee-adjusted ratio for flagged rows, q otherwise; keys P3/P4/P5 (Q0 rounded to 3/4/5 decimals) and PF4 (Q0 4 decimals + fee flag) are target-encoded exactly like the q keys (nested, smoothed).
**My independent check (fast harness: core one-hot LightGBM, lr 0.08, 450 rounds, 5-fold, same folds; use for relative comparisons only):** q keys only 0.76680 -> q + product keys **0.76987 (+0.00307)**. This confirms the notebook's gain.
**Coverage:** after the decode 99.1% of cash loans and 99.9% of revolving loans sit on a product value shared by >= 20 rows. Only 1,987 cash rows (0.9%) are unexplained and their best second fee value is the same ~127.5 -> there is no second annuity-fee layer to find.
**Credit vs goods price has a formula too, but it carries no extra risk signal:** 28.4% of cash loans have credit == goods; otherwise (credit - goods)/goods = k x 0.0132 with integer k (share of all rows: k=0 34.9%, 15: 8.4%, 12: 7.7%, 9: 6.6%, 10: 5.0%, 16: 4.5%, 8: 4.0%, 24: 3.3%; looks like insurance options).
As a key or joint key with the product it did NOT help: + k class 0.76960, + product x k 0.76925, + all joint keys 0.76907 (reference 0.76987).
**Other negative tests this round (same harness, reference 0.76987 unless noted):** numeric decoded features (Q0, fee flag, net annuity, term, net annuity/income) 0.76961; coarse backoff key (Q0 at 2 decimals) 0.76960; pooled train+test product counts 0.76972.
**Fast-harness screens against the q-only baseline 0.76680 (all keys as nested features):** exact AMT_CREDIT 0.76692 (+0.00012); exact goods price 0.76621; exact annuity 0.76640; exact income 0.76665; credit+goods pair 0.76654; DAYS_BIRTH 0.76610; fee (credit-goods) 0.76663;
q at 6 decimals 0.76662; 15 round-number/modulo features 0.76566. Nothing else behaves like q/product.
**Model capacity with fine keys (same harness, base = 11 leaves, min child 150, 450 rounds = 0.76680):** 31 leaves 0.76273; min child 50 0.76603; 31 leaves + min child 50 0.76294; 15 leaves + min child 100 + feature fraction 0.7 0.76529; 7 leaves 0.76650; 300 rounds 0.76678; 700 rounds 0.76616.
The noisy fine-resolution encodings make flexible trees overfit: keep the notebook's settings, do not add capacity. (A more-regularised direction - min child 300/600, stronger L2, lower feature fraction - was prepared but not run.)
**What this means:** the structure in annuity/credit/goods is now decoded as far as the data allows; the product keys are saturated (every extra view of them added <= 0, within noise).
**Remaining levers (all small, honest expectation 0.77715 -> about 0.778):** (1) choose the LGB/HGB blend weight from held-out predictions, not from the public board (compute the HGB model's OOF with the same folds; the shared HGB test array has no OOF in the notebook);
(2) product keys inside EVERY member (shallow LGB, XGB, CatBoost, extra seeds, 10-fold) and a fresh ensemble search; (3) re-test the template-residual blends on top of the new base (they were tuned on a weaker one); (4) the regularisation direction above.
0.78 needs +0.0029 from 0.77715; it is plausible only if several of these stack; it is not guaranteed.

## 13. Update: inner folds of the target encodings, key-set ablation, and the v4 held-out check
**Score ledger additions (from the v4 summary you shared):** v2 product keys + decoded k 0.77724; v3 greedy OOF weights **0.77746** (best, passed the held-out check 4/5 folds); v4_B (greedy, old members only) 0.77746 (same blend); v4_A (greedy, all members incl. shallow/regularized/XGBoost/CatBoost) 0.77741 (failed the 4/5 rule: better in only 2 of 5 folds, mean +0.00028 driven by two folds).
Decision rule that worked: choose weights on 4 folds, score the 5th, require >= 4/5 positive folds before spending a submission. The ensemble is saturated: four extra member types each moved the held-out blend by a few ten-thousandths or less.
**Inner folds for the nested target encodings (training rows use K inner folds; held-out/test rows use all fold-train rows).** Hypothesis: more inner folds shrink the train/test mismatch. Result (fast harness, q + product keys, 5 outer folds): K=2 0.76937, K=3 0.76946, K=4 0.76928, **K=5 0.76987**, K=10 0.76931, K=20 0.76868.
More inner folds are WORSE (K=20: -0.0012), probably the leave-one-out effect (each row's encoding excludes its own label, which trees can exploit); the curve is flat/noisy between 2 and 10. Keep K = 5.
**Key-set ablation, my LightGBM harness (reference: q only 0.76680):** F current set (q + P3,P4,P5 + product x fee) 0.76987; **E q + P3,P4,P5 0.76994 (+0.00007)**; **G product keys only 0.77010 (+0.00023)**. This agrees with the HGB ablation in your summary (fine product key carries the gain; coarse family +0.0006 only; product x fee neutral-to-slightly-harmful; q keys redundant after the decode).
Differences of 0.0002 are inside single-run noise; the optional v5 (retrain the 5 core members with E or G) is expected to be worth +0.0002 to +0.0004 for about 1.5 h of Colab - only worth it if the gap to your target is small.

## 14. Update: v6 result and the extra_trees finding (v7 notebook)
**Ledger:** v6 notebook (learned-expectation / residual features in every member; file v6_B = 50/50 mix of old and new members) reported by you as **0.778**. Target now 0.79 (rank 1-3 were 0.79101 / 0.7903 / 0.78998 in the last table I saw).
**New finding (training procedure, not a feature): `extra_trees=True` in LightGBM.** The nested target encodings are noisy; flexible trees overfit them (31 leaves: 0.76843 vs 0.76987; min child 50: 0.76603; more rounds: worse). Randomised split thresholds regularise against that.
Fast harness (core one-hot LightGBM, product keys, lr 0.08, 450 rounds, 5-fold held-out, nested encodings): reference 0.76987 -> **extra_trees 0.77065 (+0.00078)**; confirmed with another model seed and inner partition: 0.76960 -> 0.77037 (**+0.00077**);

## 15. Update: reading the v9 reproduction / v12 product-aware logs, and the v13 notebook
**Ledger:** v9 E1 multi-view (add_old_v6new_new) **0.77866**, E1 swap 0.77865, E2 resid-3-seeds 0.77855 / 0.77847, E3 pairwise ranker 0.77828, v11 family bagging 0.7784 (its baseline v6_B was 0.77836, so +0.00004: no information gained).
The reproduction notebook rebuilds the 0.77865 file exactly (Spearman 1.0, max difference 1e-16). Held-out per member: old 0.77036-0.77070, e1 (multi-view) 0.77122-0.77176 (about +0.001 each); blends old 0.77202, e1 0.77258, final 50/50 **0.77330** (mixing two variants = +0.0007 on top of the better one).
The public score is now about 0.0054 above the held-out blend (earlier offsets 0.0036-0.0042).
**v10 gate results:** NULL pooled 0.77152 vs baseline 0.77148 (+0.00004, 2/5 folds, CI [-0.00055,+0.00073]); A +0.00001; B -0.00027; both FAIL as designed, cell 7 skipped by design (not an error).
**v12 product-aware targets:** v1_allfam_oh 0.77148 -> 0.77136 (-0.00012), v2_allfam_oh 0.77124 -> 0.77170 (+0.00046); two-member average +0.00019 mean, 4/5 folds positive, pooled +0.00018, CI [-0.00038,+0.00073] -> gate fail.
**Findings (code and design):**
1. *The bootstrap gate is underpowered.* The NULL control shows the 95% interval is about +-0.0006 wide, so no gain below ~0.0006 can pass, although public steps of +0.0002 to +0.0003 do show up (E1 +0.0003, E2 +0.0002). A fail at +0.0002 means "inconclusive", not "negative". Use the nested mean + fold-positivity rule and treat the interval as information.
2. *Blend weights are stale.* The five weights (0.24/0.12/0.32/0.16/0.16) were fixed from the OLD members; the e1 members are now about +0.001 better each and nearly equal (0.7712-0.7718), so the optimum may differ. Cell 7 of v13 re-optimises them with nested CV from cached predictions (no retraining).
3. *Untested lever: extra_trees (+0.0008 to +0.0014 per member in my LightGBM held-out tests)* has not been applied to the e1 pipeline; since mixing variants was worth +0.0007, an extra_trees variant of the e1 members is the natural third component (cell 8 of v13).
4. *View-stacking train/test mismatch (not tested):* view features for training rows come from 3-fold inner models (about 53% of the rows) while validation/test rows get models trained on 100% of the fold-train rows. With target encodings, 5 inner folds beat 3 (0.76987 vs 0.76946) and 2-20 inner folds were all within noise to worse, so this is a small, unproven opportunity (try 5 inner folds in compute_views).
5. *Unequal full-data seeds:* qf_shallow and qf_reg use 5 seeds, the other three 3 seeds. More seeds reduce test-side noise that the held-out AUC cannot show (held-out uses one model per fold); +0.0002 to +0.0005 is plausible, unmeasured.
6. No leakage found in the view stacking (views for validation rows come from models trained on the fold-train rows only) or in the learned-expectation features (label-free).
**v13 (GCI_v13_reweight_and_ET_colab.ipynb):** your reproduction notebook + cell 7 (nested weight re-optimisation, ~5 min) + cell 8 (extra_trees e1 members, ~1.7 h, three-way blends B and C). Smoke-tested end to end on small data; no real numbers yet.
I'll start with a quick look at what you uploaded, and I'm launching the inner-folds experiment in the background at the same time.

I ran the experiment from your summary, and it found nothing to change. Your best file stays v3_A / v4_B at 0.77746, and there is no new CSV this turn.

**Inner folds.** The idea was that training rows see encodings built from 80% of the data while test rows see 100%, and that more inner folds might close the gap. In my held-out runs (q + product keys, 5 outer folds) it didn't work:

| Inner folds | Held-out AUC |
|---|---|
| 2 | 0.76937 |
| 3 | 0.76946 |
| 4 | 0.76928 |
| **5 (current)** | **0.76987** |
| 10 | 0.76931 |
| 20 | 0.76868 |

More folds are worse (−0.0012 at 20). My guess is the leave-one-out effect: each row's encoding excludes its own label, and trees can exploit that. Between 2 and 10 the curve is flat and noisy, so keep 5.

**Key sets.** I also tested the other assistant's ablation in LightGBM. The reference is q-only at 0.76680.

| Key set | Held-out AUC |
|---|---|
| F: current set (q + P3, P4, P5 + product × fee) | 0.76987 |
| E: q + P3, P4, P5 | 0.76994 |
| G: product keys only, no q keys | 0.77010 |

That agrees with its finding that the fine product key carries the gain, the q keys are redundant after the decode, and product × fee is neutral to slightly harmful. The differences are 0.0001 to 0.0002, which is inside single-run noise.

**On your pasted analysis.** I agree with it, and my numbers match. Your rule (choose weights on four folds, score the fifth, require at least four of five positive) correctly rejected v4_A, which scored 0.77741. The ensemble looks saturated.

# GCI World 2026 - Research Notes (everything we learned)

Last updated after the q-fingerprint submission scored **0.77283** on the public leaderboard (previous best 0.76815).
This file is written so that you (or a new chat/assistant) can continue the competition without re-doing work. Facts are separated from guesses; every number says whether it is a
held-out (leak-free) estimate or a public-leaderboard score.

## 0. TL;DR
1. **The big discovery:** every "rate-curve shape" feature (rate_std, rate_range, r24, curvatures...) is a function of ONE number, `q = AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_CREDIT`.
   The real signal is default risk as a function of `q` at fine resolution (about 800-1,500 distinct values = lender loan products). Trees cannot resolve it with default binning.
   Giving LightGBM smoothed, leak-safe target encodings of `q` (rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals) lifted a single model from 0.76265 to 0.76713 held-out, and the public score from 0.76815 to **0.77283**.
2. The held-out method (below) has predicted every public move to within about 0.0003 - trust it, not local numbers from weak models or the rounded public board.
3. Everything that is a re-expression of columns the trees already see (ratios, logs, EXT divisions, harmonic means, exact rates) is worth ~0. Gains come from information trees cannot learn: fine-resolution product identity (q), exact loan templates, more rows/seeds, diverse models.
4. 0.785 would be +0.012 over 0.77283 (about 2.6 times what the q discovery added). Nothing found so far supports promising it; the best chance is hunting for a second "hidden structure" like q (section 9).
5. Rules: only the supplied train/test/sample files. No external data/labels, no hand-labeling, no test labels. Final ranking uses the private leaderboard and your LAST submitted file. Honors/Outstanding needs code that reproduces the CSV, and AI-assisted code must be disclosed.

## 3. Data facts
- 32 predictors: 24 numeric + 8 categorical (NAME_CONTRACT_TYPE, CODE_GENDER, NAME_INCOME_TYPE, NAME_EDUCATION_TYPE, NAME_FAMILY_STATUS, NAME_HOUSING_TYPE, OCCUPATION_TYPE, ORGANIZATION_TYPE). Default rate 8.07%.
- SK_ID_CURR was re-indexed: train 0-171,201, test 171,202-232,701 (no leak in the ID; row order carries no signal, AUC 0.503). No duplicate rows within or across train/test.
- DAYS_EMPLOYED = 365243 is a sentinel (18% of train rows; default 5.4% vs 8.7%). Missingness: EXT_SOURCE_1 69.5%, OWN_CAR_AGE 66%, EXT_SOURCE_3 31.9%; missingness itself carries signal.
- No major train/test shift (PSI < 0.005 on top features; adversarial AUC about 0.516). EXT_SOURCE_1/2/3 are the strongest raw features (univariate AUC 0.66-0.68).
- Only 0.3% of rows share person-level fields (birth, registration, ID dates); the same-person link is real (0.88 AUC inside groups) but covers too few rows to matter (+0.0001).

## 4. The q-fingerprint discovery (details)
**Why it works.** For an amortizing loan, ANNUITY/CREDIT = r(1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1). For any fixed term n, the implied rate is a strictly increasing function of q = ANNUITY/CREDIT, so all rate/curvature/dispersion features computed from q are functions of q alone
(R^2 of each shape feature against a 400-bin function of q: 0.9993-1.0000). They add nothing to a tree that already has q (the exact-rate features alone gave +0.0002 when models were trained properly).
What matters is that **risk varies sharply across specific q values**: q rounded to 4 decimals has about 814 distinct values and every row shares its value with at least 5 others - lender products (rate x term combinations).
LightGBM's default 255 bins and regularization cannot resolve this; a smoothed lookup of default rate by q scores 0.675 AUC alone (earlier exact-value fingerprints ~0.62).
**How it is built (leak-safe).** For each key (q rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals): TE = (sum_y + m0 * prior) / (count + m0) with m0 = 100, 30, 30, plus the count. Training rows use inner 5-fold out-of-fold encodings (seed 7);
validation/test rows use all rows of the training portion only. Outer folds: StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle, seed 42).
**Evidence (all held-out, same folds).**
- As a blend member only: +0.00125 at 10% weight (+0.00111 at 7%, +0.00101 at 15%, -0.0034 at 30%); choosing the best member on 4 folds and scoring the 5th: +0.00118, 5/5 folds positive.
- As FEATURES inside LightGBM (the strong version): 0.76713 vs 0.76265 without (+0.00448). Features beat the blend by 4x.
- Three models: qf_onehot 0.76713, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678, qf_mono 0.76746; average 0.76843 (previous best recipe 0.76458); recipe + average at W=0.7: 0.76861 (+0.00403); W chosen on 4 folds scored on the 5th: +0.00408, 5/5 positive; optimum flat for W in 0.7-1.0.
- Resolution matters: 182 distinct values +0.00102 (blend test); 814 values +0.00125; 1,493 values +0.00127. Shrinkage does not (m0 10-60 all about +0.0012).
- Other keys as extra features gave nothing (first 4 of 5 folds, change vs q-only: +0.00009, +0.00008, -0.00014, -0.00052): goods/credit (3 and 4 decimals), credit/income, annuity/income, q x goods ratio, q x contract type. q is special.
- As blend members, only q x contract type added anything among 12 customer states; the others hurt.
**Prediction vs reality.** Held-out predicted +0.0040; public gave +0.0047 (0.76815 -> 0.77283).
**Why the rate-shape/RD3 chain only moved +0.0001-0.0002 per step:** it was a coarse, 1-D, weak-anchored approximation of this signal, applied as a tiny blend.

## 5. Validation method (use this, and its traps)
- Held-out = every training row predicted by a model that never saw it (5-fold, seed 42). Fixed boosting rounds chosen WITHOUT the held-out labels (early stopping on the held-out fold is optimistic; it inflated an early pseudo-label "gain" to +0.0008 when the clean result was -0.0014).
- Target-derived features must be nested (inner out-of-fold for training rows). Never use in-sample predictions to build residuals.
- A correction fitted against a WEAK anchor model does not transfer: RD3 (anchor weaker than the champion), model-residual templates (fast base 0.759), EXT-division/rate models with under-trained baselines all showed "+0.003" locally and ~0 or negative publicly/against a strong base.
- A pre-registered test: choose weights/members on 4 folds, score on the 5th, repeat; need 5/5 positive. Public noise (0.0001-0.0005) is larger than most single ideas - decide with held-out, confirm with ONE public submission.
- test.csv has no labels; only the leaderboard scores test predictions. Do not loop on the public board.

**Research suggesting NNs:** RealMLP/TabM with tuning+ensembling can match GBDTs and blend well on 1K-500K rows (Holzmueller et al., NeurIPS 2024 https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04491; TabArena https://alphaxiv.org/abs/2506.16791). Not tested here (no torch/GPU): see notebook section 4.

## 9. Research plan beyond 0.77283 (ranked; every item untested unless stated)
Use the notebook's `test_extra_key` (5-fold, leak-free, ~10 min) to test candidates; only a gain above ~+0.0003 with the same folds is worth a public submission.
1. **Hunt for a second "hidden structure" like q.** q worked because lenders generate loan terms by formula. Look for other values generated by formulas/catalogs: exact AMT_CREDIT, AMT_GOODS_PRICE, AMT_ANNUITY, AMT_INCOME_TOTAL values as keys (as FEATURES, not blends); round-number patterns (value mod 1,000 / 5,000 / 9,000, last digits, multiples of 4.5); annuity per month vs goods price; DAYS_BIRTH as a key.
2. **Re-do the whole ensemble with q-features inside every member** (shallow LGB, extra seeds, XGBoost, CatBoost) and re-run the 25-step ensemble search; then re-test the residual-template and fingerprint blends against this stronger base (they were tuned against a weaker one and may now overlap).
3. **Tune the q-models now that they carry more information:** more rounds/leaves, lower learning rate, 10-fold, 8+ seeds, full-data refits (the seed and learning curves say +0.001 or more).
4. **Pairwise error analysis against the NEW champion** with strict out-of-fold predictions (notebook gives OOF): where does the champion rank positives below negatives? Group by q bin, contract type, EXT missingness, product groups.
5. **Higher-resolution q keys** (round 6 decimals; hierarchical backoff) and q-conditioned encodings as features for sub-populations (contract type was the only helpful state as a blend; others hurt).
6. **NN diversity (RealMLP/TabM) on Colab GPU with the q-features** - earlier NNs were weak (0.745) and blend-negative; low expectation, listed for completeness.
7. Do NOT repeat: harmonic EXT, exact-rate/EXT-division features, pseudo-labelling, adaptive gating, KNN residuals, global meta-models, weighted loss, extra ratio keys (goods/credit, credit/income, annuity/income).
**Honest expectation:** 0.785 needs about +0.012. The q discovery was worth +0.0047 and came from asking "what structure generates these numbers?". Another discovery of similar size is possible but not guaranteed; do not plan around it.
