## 12. Update: product-key decode (your 0.77715 notebook), verification, and what is left
**Scores added to the ledger:** the Colab notebook output (3 q-feature LightGBM models + our blend) **0.77321**; the product-key notebook blend **0.77715** (+0.0039). Held-out numbers in the product-key notebook as reported by you
(not recomputed by me except the check below): qf_onehot 0.76713 -> 0.76992, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678 -> 0.77070, qf_mono 0.76746 -> 0.77035, 3-member average 0.76843 -> 0.77158.
**The decode (label-free):** annuity = Q0 x credit + F, where F is a fixed monthly fee (about 129.5; a plateau of 125.5-130.5 scores the same) on about 75.1% of cash loans. A row is flagged as fee-bearing when (annuity - 129.5)/credit has more rows sharing its 5-decimal value than annuity/credit does.
Q0 = fee-adjusted ratio for flagged rows, q otherwise; keys P3/P4/P5 (Q0 rounded to 3/4/5 decimals) and PF4 (Q0 4 decimals + fee flag) are target-encoded exactly like the q keys (nested, smoothed).
**My independent check (fast harness: core one-hot LightGBM, lr 0.08, 450 rounds, 5-fold, same folds; use for relative comparisons only):** q keys only 0.76680 -> q + product keys **0.76987 (+0.00307)**. This confirms the notebook's gain.
**Coverage:** after the decode 99.1% of cash loans and 99.9% of revolving loans sit on a product value shared by >= 20 rows. Only 1,987 cash rows (0.9%) are unexplained and their best second fee value is the same ~127.5 -> there is no second annuity-fee layer to find.
**Credit vs goods price has a formula too, but it carries no extra risk signal:** 28.4% of cash loans have credit == goods; otherwise (credit - goods)/goods = k x 0.0132 with integer k (share of all rows: k=0 34.9%, 15: 8.4%, 12: 7.7%, 9: 6.6%, 10: 5.0%, 16: 4.5%, 8: 4.0%, 24: 3.3%; looks like insurance options).
As a key or joint key with the product it did NOT help: + k class 0.76960, + product x k 0.76925, + all joint keys 0.76907 (reference 0.76987).
**Other negative tests this round (same harness, reference 0.76987 unless noted):** numeric decoded features (Q0, fee flag, net annuity, term, net annuity/income) 0.76961; coarse backoff key (Q0 at 2 decimals) 0.76960; pooled train+test product counts 0.76972.
**Fast-harness screens against the q-only baseline 0.76680 (all keys as nested features):** exact AMT_CREDIT 0.76692 (+0.00012); exact goods price 0.76621; exact annuity 0.76640; exact income 0.76665; credit+goods pair 0.76654; DAYS_BIRTH 0.76610; fee (credit-goods) 0.76663;
q at 6 decimals 0.76662; 15 round-number/modulo features 0.76566. Nothing else behaves like q/product.
**Model capacity with fine keys (same harness, base = 11 leaves, min child 150, 450 rounds = 0.76680):** 31 leaves 0.76273; min child 50 0.76603; 31 leaves + min child 50 0.76294; 15 leaves + min child 100 + feature fraction 0.7 0.76529; 7 leaves 0.76650; 300 rounds 0.76678; 700 rounds 0.76616.
The noisy fine-resolution encodings make flexible trees overfit: keep the notebook's settings, do not add capacity. (A more-regularised direction - min child 300/600, stronger L2, lower feature fraction - was prepared but not run.)
**What this means:** the structure in annuity/credit/goods is now decoded as far as the data allows; the product keys are saturated (every extra view of them added <= 0, within noise).
**Remaining levers (all small, honest expectation 0.77715 -> about 0.778):** (1) choose the LGB/HGB blend weight from held-out predictions, not from the public board (compute the HGB model's OOF with the same folds; the shared HGB test array has no OOF in the notebook);
(2) product keys inside EVERY member (shallow LGB, XGB, CatBoost, extra seeds, 10-fold) and a fresh ensemble search; (3) re-test the template-residual blends on top of the new base (they were tuned on a weaker one); (4) the regularisation direction above.
0.78 needs +0.0029 from 0.77715; it is plausible only if several of these stack; it is not guaranteed.

## 13. Update: inner folds of the target encodings, key-set ablation, and the v4 held-out check
**Score ledger additions (from the v4 summary you shared):** v2 product keys + decoded k 0.77724; v3 greedy OOF weights **0.77746** (best, passed the held-out check 4/5 folds); v4_B (greedy, old members only) 0.77746 (same blend); v4_A (greedy, all members incl. shallow/regularized/XGBoost/CatBoost) 0.77741 (failed the 4/5 rule: better in only 2 of 5 folds, mean +0.00028 driven by two folds).
Decision rule that worked: choose weights on 4 folds, score the 5th, require >= 4/5 positive folds before spending a submission. The ensemble is saturated: four extra member types each moved the held-out blend by a few ten-thousandths or less.

Objective: Test whether a pairwise ranking objective contributes complementary ranking signal to the existing tuned five-member LightGBM ensemble. This is a bounded experiment, not a new broad model search. Do not rerun automated formula discovery: 364 formula candidates have already failed to produce a reliable gain.
1. Freeze the evaluation

* Use the existing outer-fold assignments and the same held-out rows used to evaluate the incumbent ensemble.
* Record the incumbent's pooled OOF predictions before training anything new.
* Do not use public leaderboard results, the outer validation folds, or the final holdout to choose hyperparameters, training rounds, or blend weights.
* Keep all decisions and random seeds in the experiment log.

2. Train the ranker without leakage
Use `XGBRanker` with:

* `objective="rank:pairwise"`
* `tree_method="hist"`
* `lambdarank_pair_method="mean"`
* Start with `lambdarank_num_pair_per_sample=1`.
* Use a deliberately small, predeclared configuration rather than a large hyperparameter search.

Treat every outer-training partition as one global query group. Pass one group size equal to the number of training rows to the ranker. Do not create arbitrary groups by customer segment or product.
Keep the current validated feature set, including the decoded product feature. Reuse the established leakage-safe product-encoding pipeline:

* Every outer-training row's product target encoding must exclude that row's label; use the existing nested/OOF encoding procedure.
* Outer-validation encodings must be learned from the outer-training labels only.
* Do not use validation or test labels to create encodings, pairs, groups, or features.

Choose the number of boosting rounds using inner validation within the outer-training partition, scoring candidates by ROC-AUC. Refit the outer-fold ranker with the selected round count, then predict the untouched outer-validation rows. Do not select rounds from outer-fold results.
3. Test ranking performance and ensemble diversity separately
Save row-aligned OOF predictions for the ranker and incumbent ensemble.
Report all three results:

1. Ranker-only pooled OOF ROC-AUC.
2. Incumbent ensemble pooled OOF ROC-AUC.
3. Incumbent-plus-ranker blend pooled OOF ROC-AUC.

Use the existing non-negative linear blending procedure, with weights selected strictly inside the training data through nested CV. Do not fit a new flexible meta-learner on the same OOF predictions used to claim the improvement. Standardize score scales using training-only statistics when needed for blending; apply those same statistics to validation/test predictions.
Do not assume the ranker's standalone AUC must beat the incumbent: the question is whether its errors contain complementary signal.
4. Apply the existing pass/fail gate

* Compare the incumbent and blended predictions on identical held-out rows.
* Calculate the AUC difference and a paired, stratified bootstrap confidence interval on pooled held-out predictions.
* Report per-fold results as diagnostics, not as five independent samples for a naive t-test.
* Retain the candidate only if the improvement is credible under the existing paired-bootstrap gate, reasonably stable across folds, and achieved with a non-trivial selected blend weight.
* Otherwise record the result and stop. Do not respond to a failed test by launching a broad XGBoost tuning search.

Save the configuration, library version, fold assignments, selected iteration counts, standalone and blended OOF predictions, bootstrap interval, runtime, and final pass/fail decision in `experiments_log.md`.
Do not report a predicted AUC gain before the run. No improvement is established until the held-out results exist.
# Domain-driven research: finding new credit-risk signal in your 32 columns

I focused this investigation on a different question: what would a credit underwriter infer about an applicant that your current LightGBM might not represent efficiently?

The strongest lead comes from combining your decoded loan-product structure with actual underwriting concepts: repayment capacity, the economics of the loan contract, recent credit-seeking behaviour, and observed delinquency in an applicant's social circle.

I also checked the original Home Credit competition's winning solution. It specifically identified the credit-to-annuity relationship and a derived interest-rate feature as valuable, while emphasising that many of its largest gains depended on historical credit and payment tables. Those historical-table features are unavailable to you, so copying the original winning recipe would not solve your problem.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.kaggle.com\&sz=32)

Kaggle

+1



One important distinction: I found defensible domain mechanisms and historical precedents, but no published experiment proving an incremental AUC gain against your particular 32-column model. I haven't run your local data. The objective is to identify a small, technically precise experiment that could reveal new signal—not to promise an unmeasured improvement.

## 1. Highest-priority idea: separate the economics of the loan from the borrower's ability to pay

Your decoded relationship is unusually useful here. For the product families where your reconstruction holds, you found approximately:

\\[ A_i \approx q\_{p(i)}C_i+f\_{p(i)} \\]

where \\(A_i\\) is the monthly annuity, \\(C_i\\) is credit amount, \\(q\_{p(i)}\\) is the product-specific payment factor, and \\(f\_{p(i)}\\) is the fixed payment component.

The domain insight is that these quantities describe different things:

- \\(q_p\\) describes how much monthly payment is attached to each unit of principal under the observed contract structure.
- \\(f_p\\) captures a fixed product-related payment component where the relationship is valid.
- The borrower's income determines whether the resulting payment is affordable.

This fits the standard underwriting distinction between debt-to-income and residual income: a payment relative to income is one signal, while the income remaining after paying the loan is another.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.consumerfinance.gov\&sz=32)

Consumer Financial Protection Bureau

+1



### The experiment

Rather than creating another product target encoding, use your existing product decoder to construct a contract-adjusted affordability block.

Define annual income as \\(Y_i\\). Assuming the dataset's annuity is a monthly payment and income is annual, construct:

\\[ \text{Monthly residual}\_i=\frac{Y_i}{12}-A_i \\]

\\[ \text{Principal-linked DSR}\_i= \frac{12q\_{p(i)}C_i}{Y_i} \\]

\\[ \text{Fixed-fee burden}\_i= \frac{12f\_{p(i)}}{Y_i} \\]

\\[ \text{Fee share}\_i=\frac{f\_{p(i)}}{A_i} \\]

The principal-linked debt-service ratio and fixed-fee burden should approximately sum to the annualised payment burden when the decoded equation holds. The new information is not a new arithmetic ratio by itself; it is the decomposition into a product-specific payment factor and a fixed component, potentially making the contract economics easier for the model to represent.

Do not call \\(q_p\\) an interest rate or APR. The payment factor alone does not identify an interest rate because the repayment term is also needed.

### Why this is genuinely different from your earlier product experiments

Your product-key target encoding learns average default risk for each decoded product. This experiment asks a different question: how does the pricing structure of that product translate into financial pressure for this specific applicant?

That is a borrower-capacity interaction, not product-by-segment target encoding, product neighbour smoothing, or a new estimate of product default risk.

There is a strict condition: only apply the decomposition to product families where the affine relationship is validated. You found the fixed-fee relationship holds for roughly 75% of cash-loan cases, not universally. For other products, leave the derived values missing and add a supported/unsupported flag; do not force the same formula onto revolving or otherwise different contracts.

### Minimal PyTorch/LightGBM-compatible feature implementation

You don't need PyTorch for this feature block. It is deterministic domain preprocessing, so you can feed the features into your existing LightGBM model or any PyTorch or TensorFlow tabular model.

```
import numpy as npimport pandas as pddef add_contract_affordability_features(    df: pd.DataFrame,    product_q: pd.Series,    product_fee: pd.Series,    contract_valid: pd.Series,) -> pd.DataFrame:    """Feature block based on the decoded annuity = q * credit + fee relation.    product_q, product_fee and contract_valid must align row-for-row.    contract_valid should be False where the decoded relation is unsupported.    """    out = df.copy()    credit = pd.to_numeric(out["AMT_CREDIT"], errors="coerce")    annuity = pd.to_numeric(out["AMT_ANNUITY"], errors="coerce")    income = pd.to_numeric(out["AMT_INCOME_TOTAL"], errors="coerce")    q = pd.Series(product_q, index=out.index, dtype=float)    fee = pd.Series(product_fee, index=out.index, dtype=float)    valid = (        pd.Series(contract_valid, index=out.index).fillna(False).astype(bool)        & credit.gt(0)        & annuity.gt(0)        & income.gt(0)        & q.notna()        & fee.notna()    )    # Defined for all rows where income and annuity are valid.    out["GROSS_MONTHLY_RESIDUAL_AFTER_LOAN"] = (        income / 12.0 - annuity    ).where(income.gt(0) & annuity.notna())
```

The function deliberately doesn't fabricate a decoded fee for unsupported products or replace a missing value with zero. It also doesn't add a contract residual by default: if the equation is almost exact by construction, that residual would contain little or no information.

## 2. A second, potentially independent domain signal: recent credit-seeking acceleration

If the 32 columns include `AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_WEEK`, `AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_MON`, `AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_QRT`, and `AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_YEAR`, test the timing of credit enquiries, not another total-count feature.

The Home Credit application table records bureau enquiry counts at several timescales. Public Home Credit feature-engineering references explicitly discuss both these fields and the social-circle delinquency fields.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



The underwriting idea is that an applicant with a high annual enquiry count may have a long history of credit shopping, while another applicant may have a disproportionate share of enquiries concentrated in the most recent week. Those patterns need not mean the same thing.

For a seven-day window, define:

\\[ B_7 = \log\left( \frac{N_7+0.5} {N\_{365}(7/365)+0.5} \right) \\]

Here \\(N_7\\) is the recent-week count and \\(N\_{365}\\) is the annual count. The denominator is a simple steady-rate expectation for the recent week; the feature measures how unusual the recent count is relative to that expectation.

Similarly, define \\(B\_{30}\\) and \\(B\_{90}\\) using the monthly and quarterly counts. These are velocity or concentration proxies, not probabilities of default. A positive value means the recent window contains more enquiries than the simple steady-rate reference would suggest.

Why use logs and a small stabilising constant? Enquiry counts are often zero or small, making raw ratios unstable. Why not just sum every time window? Because short and long windows may overlap; summing them can count the same activity more than once.

```
def add_bureau_inquiry_velocity(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:    out = df.copy()    windows = [        ("WEEK", 7),        ("MON", 30),        ("QRT", 90),    ]    year_col = "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_YEAR"    if year_col not in out.columns:        return out    year_count = pd.to_numeric(out[year_col], errors="coerce")    for suffix, days in windows:        recent_col = f"AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_{suffix}"        if recent_col not in out.columns:            continue        recent = pd.to_numeric(out[recent_col], errors="coerce")        expected = year_count * (days / 365.0)        # Missing counts stay missing; they are not silently treated as zero.        out[f"BUREAU_ENQUIRY_BURST_{days}D"] = np.log(            (recent + 0.5) / (expected + 0.5)        )    available_cols = [        c for c in [            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_HOUR",            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_DAY",            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_WEEK",            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_MON",            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_QRT",            "AMT_REQ_CREDIT_BUREAU_YEAR",
```

There is a caveat here: enquiries can reflect comparison shopping as well as financial pressure. Consumer-credit guidance recognises that credit shopping can produce multiple inquiries in a short period, so I would not impose a monotonic rule that every burst must mean higher risk. Let the model estimate the direction.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.consumerfinance.gov\&sz=32)

Consumer Financial Protection Bureau

+1



This is worth testing only if those columns are actually in your 32-column subset and haven't already been represented with an equivalent feature.

## 3. A third domain signal, if available: shrink noisy social-circle delinquency rates

The Home Credit application data can contain counts of observed social-circle defaults at 30-day and 60-day delinquency thresholds, alongside counts of observable people.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



The raw default count is not comparable across people when one applicant has only a few observed peers and another has many. This suggests a more principled feature than dividing two integers without accounting for how noisy the denominator is.

For each threshold, let \\(d_i\\) be the number of delinquent observed peers and \\(n_i\\) the number observed. Construct the shrunken rate

\\[ \hat p_i=\frac{d_i+k p_0}{n_i+k} \\]

where \\(p_0\\) is the pooled peer delinquency rate estimated from the training fold's feature counts only, and \\(k\\) is a predeclared small prior strength, such as 5.

This is not target encoding the current applicant's outcome. The inputs are the social-circle counts themselves; neither the applicant's `TARGET` nor their product risk is used to construct the feature.

For the first test, create the 30-day and 60-day posterior rates, plus the number of observed people. If there are enough observations, also retain a posterior uncertainty measure so the model can distinguish a noisy rate based on few observations from one supported by a larger observed group.

Your earlier negative result for hierarchical product-key shrinkage does not establish that this different operation will fail: here the quantity being stabilised is a measured count rate with a known denominator, not a category's estimated default risk. However, there is no reliable evidence that this will improve AUC on your particular subset, so it should remain a separate, small ablation.

## 4. Turn this into a controlled research programme

After 70 experiments, I would not add all these features at once. You need to know which domain hypothesis adds information and which just creates another redundant representation of a column.

## A

First: contract-adjusted affordability

This is the most dataset-specific idea because it uses your discovered loan-product equation. Test its four features as a single block against your incumbent and keep the existing product target encoding unchanged.

## B

Second: inquiry acceleration

Only test this if the relevant columns exist and no equivalent burst feature has already been tested. This is a distinct behavioural hypothesis rather than another loan-amount ratio.

## C

Third: shrunken peer delinquency rates

This is conditional on the social-circle fields being present. Estimate the smoothing prior from each training fold's counts alone, and compare the rate features with and without the uncertainty feature.

### The exact AUC decision rule

For each block, train your existing model configuration on the same five outer folds. Save row-aligned OOF predictions for the baseline and candidate; don't retune the entire model to rescue a new feature block.

Then:

1. Calculate the difference in pooled OOF ROC-AUC and a paired, stratified-bootstrap 95% confidence interval.
2. Record each fold's change as a stability diagnostic, not as five independent samples for a t-test.
3. Retain a feature block only if the improvement is credible and practically meaningful. If multiple blocks pass individually, test their combination once.
4. Confirm any shortlisted candidate on a final, frozen holdout before using it in the final model. This is prospective confirmation, although earlier experimentation means it cannot be described as a completely untouched historical holdout.

Do not select these features using public leaderboard results.

## My conclusion

The first experiment I would actually run is the contract-adjusted affordability block built from your decoded product coefficients and fee components. It directly links your strongest discovered structure to a recognised credit-underwriting concept: the burden a specific loan contract places on a specific applicant.

The enquiry-velocity and shrunken social-circle features are worthwhile only if their columns are present in your subset and haven't already been tested. They explore different domain mechanisms: short-term credit-seeking behaviour and observed peer delinquency.

Published Home Credit solutions show that contract-related features have been valuable historically, and credit-underwriting guidance supports considering both payment burden and residual income. Neither establishes a new AUC gain on your specific data.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.kaggle.com\&sz=32)

Kaggle

+1



That is the honest next step: test a few carefully derived domain features against fixed held-out predictions, and let measured AUC—not the sophistication of the idea—decide whether they survive.&#x20;
# Advanced research report: improving your Home Credit ROC-AUC with PyTorch and TensorFlow

Your problem is unusually well-defined now: 32 application-only columns, 171,202 training rows, around 8.07% defaults, and a tuned five-member LightGBM ensemble at approximately 0.772 cross-validation AUC. You have already tried roughly 70 experiments, including broad feature engineering, category encodings, unsupervised features, and neural-network blends.

I looked for methods that change the training objective, representation, or way a neural model uses its inputs, rather than another conventional architecture or generic feature search.

Two research directions stand out: a trainable neural model explicitly optimised for AUC, and a new tabular foundation model with an unusually sophisticated inference-time ensemble.

## 1. Strongest practical training experiment: TabM + direct AUC optimisation

Highest-priority trainable experiment

### Why this is more advanced than a neural-network blend

TabM, published at ICLR 2025, uses shared weights to train multiple neural predictors efficiently. Instead of fitting unrelated MLPs and averaging them afterward, it trains a parameter-efficient ensemble in parallel. The official implementation is PyTorch-based.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



There is also relevant, if imperfect, Home Credit evidence: a public TabReD benchmark currently reports ROC-AUC of 0.8737 for TabM and 0.8728 for XGBoost on its 1.1-million-row, 696-feature temporal dataset. The difference is just 0.0009, and the data and evaluation protocol differ substantially from yours. It is evidence that this model family can be competitive on a Home Credit task—not evidence of the gain you should expect from your subset.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.sota2.com\&sz=32)

sota2.com



The additional technique is fine-tuning with an AUC-specific surrogate loss, rather than relying on ordinary binary cross-entropy throughout training.

A research benchmark comparing ten deep AUROC losses found that composite AUC losses were more competitive than conventional pairwise losses on most of its tested datasets. It also found that the positive-example sampling rate can materially affect generalisation, while an optimizer that looks best during training may not be best on unseen data. The experiments included image and molecular data, not your Home Credit subset, so this supports a testable training strategy rather than a guaranteed credit-scoring improvement.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+2



The library [LibAUC](https://libauc.org/) provides a PyTorch implementation of AUC-margin optimisation and the PESG optimizer. Its documented workflow recommends first training with cross-entropy, then fine-tuning with the AUC surrogate.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://libauc.org\&sz=32)

Machine Learning Library for AUC Optimization.

+1



### The proposed training design

Phase A — Learn a strong starting representation

Train TabM using binary cross-entropy on the fold's training rows. Use only the features and product-encoding pipeline already validated in your experiments.

Phase B — Fine-tune for positive-negative separation

Replace the cross-entropy objective with an AUC-margin loss, using the corresponding LibAUC optimizer rather than simply swapping the loss and retaining an arbitrary training configuration.

Phase C — Test complementary ranking information

Compare the fine-tuned TabM against both the original TabM and the existing LightGBM ensemble. Only evaluate a blend if its weight is selected inside the training data.

The reason to separate these phases is that cross-entropy is useful for learning the initial signal, while the second phase puts more direct pressure on positive scores being higher than negative scores. AUC-margin optimisation is a surrogate for the ranking metric, not the exact non-differentiable ROC-AUC calculation itself.

### Details I would lock before running

| Component                             | First prespecified setting                                                                                                                                |
| ------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Architecture                          | Official PyTorch TabM implementation                                                                                                                      |
| Initial objective                     | Binary cross-entropy                                                                                                                                      |
| Fine-tuning objective                 | LibAUC AUC-margin loss                                                                                                                                    |
| Optimizer                             | The compatible PESG implementation recommended by LibAUC                                                                                                  |
| Positive fraction in training batches | Start at 20%; test 30% only as a predefined sensitivity check                                                                                             |
| Features                              | Existing approved numeric features; product key represented categorically, with any target-encoded version generated by your existing nested OOF pipeline |
| Validation                            | Inner validation determines the fine-tuning duration; outer fold remains untouched                                                                        |
| Primary comparison                    | TabM with cross-entropy versus the same TabM after AUC fine-tuning                                                                                        |

Why start at a 20% positive fraction? Your natural rate is about 8.07%, so this gives the minority class more representation in each training batch. LibAUC documents a dual sampler for controlling the positive fraction, and the AUROC-loss benchmarking study reports that higher positive sampling can help—but this is not universally true, which is why I would keep the sensitivity check small.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



Important: use the AUC-tuned model's actual held-out predictions to judge success. A lower training loss or higher training AUC is not evidence that held-out AUC improved.

## 2. The newest major direction: TabFM+ with architecture-aware feature views

Highest-novelty candidate

A new research paper dated September 29, 2026 introduces TabFM, a 400-million-parameter tabular foundation model pretrained on synthetic tables. It uses in-context prediction: rather than fitting its weights to your task, it reads labeled examples and predicts new rows. The paper reports results on TabArena's 51 datasets, including 38 classification datasets, using repeated 10-fold evaluations.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



This is different from the TabICLv2/retrieval direction in our previous research. The key additional method is TabFM+, which keeps the pretrained weights frozen and generates multiple transformed views of the same table.

### What TabFM+ actually changes

The published method uses 32 views. Its feature transformations include:

- Random multiplicative cross-features: create a small selection of pairwise products among numeric columns.
- Low-rank structural features: one-hot encode categorical columns alongside standardized numeric columns and derive truncated-SVD features.
- Alternative numeric preprocessing: compare standard scaling with Yeo–Johnson power transforms and clipping extreme standardized values at approximately four standard deviations.
- Column and category permutations: generate multiple model views by changing input orderings. This matters because TabFM's input encoding is not fully invariant to column order.
- An ensemble of the resulting views: learn non-negative combination weights using internal validation predictions, shrinking the final weights toward uniform weights to discourage overfitting.&#x20;

  [image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

  arxiv.org



This deserves attention even though some cross-features, PCA-like features and formula searches already failed with your boosted trees. The same transformation can have different value for a different model's input representation. The point is not to retry these features on LightGBM; it is to reproduce the transformations as part of the TabFM+ procedure and test the resulting predictor.

### What the paper measured

Base TabFM

# 1768.6

Classification Elo

TabFM+

# 1838.0

Classification Elo

Reported difference: +69.4 Elo points across the benchmark's classification tasks. This is an aggregate benchmark rating, not a +0.0694 increase in ROC-AUC or a result on your dataset.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



The paper also presents TabFM-Auto, which uses an LLM-guided search to generate and evaluate dataset-specific processing pipelines around the frozen model. It improves 41 of 51 datasets, with the largest overall classification ranking improvement among the methods reported in that paper.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



Given your 364 failed formula candidates, I would not start with another broad formula search. I would first try TabFM+ with its prescribed view ensemble. TabFM-Auto is a separate optional experiment, but it introduces an external LLM dependency and another adaptive search process; it should be considered only if course rules allow it and any data-sharing implications are acceptable.

### Important implementation details

The [official TabFM repository](https://github.com/google-research/tabfm) supports a PyTorch backend and JAX. It does not document an official TensorFlow backend. The repository's example estimator defaults to only 100 context rows, whereas the paper's pretrained model was trained with context sizes up to 16,384 rows. Make sure any experiment deliberately configures its context limit rather than silently using the 100-row default.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



For your 171,202-row training set, I would test a context cap of 16,384 rows, sampling only labeled examples from the relevant training partition. Do not try to feed all 171,202 training rows into one context. Process validation and test queries in batches to manage memory, and use multiple views only after confirming a single-view prediction works correctly.

One licensing caveat: the official repository says the source code is Apache-2.0 licensed, but its default pretrained weights have a separate non-commercial license. Check that your course permits these weights before using them.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub



## 3. PyTorch versus TensorFlow: what actually matters for AUC

A framework does not intrinsically increase ROC-AUC. The useful distinction is which mature model and optimisation implementations are available.

PyTorch — preferred for the first experiment

Official implementations exist for TabM and LibAUC's AUC-oriented losses. This makes it the most straightforward path for testing a neural architecture and an AUC-specific objective together.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



Primary route: TabM → cross-entropy warm-up → AUC-margin fine-tuning → outer-fold predictions.

TensorFlow / Keras — viable, but requires more custom work

TensorFlow Ranking supplies pairwise ranking losses, including pairwise hinge loss. However, `tf.keras.metrics.AUC` is an evaluation metric that approximates ROC or PR AUC using thresholds; it is not a direct differentiable loss to substitute for binary cross-entropy.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.tensorflow.org\&sz=32)

tensorflow\.org

+1



Alternative route: Keras model → cross-entropy warm-up → custom AUC-surrogate loss using GradientTape, or TensorFlow Ranking with balanced batches containing both classes.

If you already have a TensorFlow pipeline, you can implement the AUC-surrogate objective there. But I would not port TabM or TabFM solely for a presumed framework advantage. Start with the ecosystem that has the most direct support for the technique you want to test.

For reference, the composite AUC loss studied in the deep-AUROC benchmark uses two trainable reference scores \\(a\\) and \\(b\\), class-conditional score penalties, and a differentiable separation penalty:

\\[ L = \mathbb{E}\_{+}[(s-a)^2] +\mathbb{E}\_{-}[(s-b)^2] +\log(1+\exp(\gamma(b-a))) \\]

Here, \\(s\\) is the model score and \\(\gamma\\) controls the smooth separation penalty. The loss is a surrogate for ranking performance, not exact ROC-AUC. Its auxiliary variables and training procedure must be implemented correctly; simply plugging this expression into a standard `compile()` call without handling those variables is not enough.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



## 4. The experiment plan I would actually commit to

After 70 experiments, I would cap the next round at two new primary candidates, with no broad parameter sweep.

## 1

TabM with AUC-margin fine-tuning

This is the practical trainable experiment. The direct AUC loss and controlled positive sampling are the new factors, not another generic neural blend. Use the same outer splits and features as the incumbent.

## 2

TabFM+ with its 32-view procedure

This is the higher-novelty foundation-model experiment. Start with the official pretrained model and the published transformation/ensemble procedure; do not combine it with a separate automated formula search in the first run.

## 3

Blend only after measuring complementary ranking errors

For each candidate, count how many positive-negative pairs the incumbent misranks but the candidate orders correctly, and how many correctly ordered pairs the candidate breaks. This reveals whether a candidate has complementary ranking signal rather than merely a different-looking probability distribution.

### Evaluation safeguards

Use the same held-out rows for the incumbent and each new candidate. Inside every outer training partition, use inner validation to select the number of training epochs, preprocessing configuration and any ensemble weights. Generate OOF predictions for the entire training set using models that did not train on the corresponding rows.

For the final comparisons, calculate paired, stratified-bootstrap intervals for the difference in ROC-AUC. Report each fold separately as a stability diagnostic, but do not treat five folds sharing training data as five independent observations in a t-test.

Because the experiment history is already extensive, freeze both candidates before the next run. A new 15% holdout can provide useful prospective confirmation, but it cannot erase the effect of earlier experimentation if its labels have indirectly influenced previous design choices. Avoid public-leaderboard selection entirely.

## 5. What I would not prioritise

I also checked TabPack, another 2026 ICML method that trains an ensemble of MLPs with varied hyperparameters in one run. Its method is interesting, but the available Home Credit TabReD benchmark reports 0.8693 ROC-AUC for TabPack, below the listed 0.8737 for TabM and 0.8728 for XGBoost in that evaluation. Given your previous neural-blend failures, I would not spend the next experiment budget on TabPack before testing the two more targeted ideas above. These results use the full TabReD dataset and a different protocol, so they are directional evidence only.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://proceedings.mlr.press\&sz=32)

proceedings.mlr.press

+1



## Final assessment

There is no research-backed way to promise that either candidate will lift your current 0.772 AUC. The evidence is nevertheless stronger and more specific than "try another model": the AUC-optimisation literature supports testing a different training objective, TabM has encouraging results on a separate Home Credit benchmark, and TabFM+ has a documented multi-view procedure that improved aggregate classification benchmark rankings.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+2



My recommendation: run the PyTorch TabM + AUC-loss experiment first, because it uses all your training labels in an ordinary supervised pipeline and is easier to evaluate cleanly. Then test TabFM+ if pretrained weights are permitted. Retain neither unless it produces a repeatable gain in held-out ROC-AUC and shows complementary positive-negative ranking errors against your existing ensemble.

That is the research-supported path I would commit to—not a predicted score, and not another 20-experiment search.
I pushed further into the newer research, including two 2026 papers that go beyond ordinary tabular model comparisons. The most promising direction now is to adapt how a pretrained tabular model retrieves and uses labeled applicants—not to engineer more ratios or train another conventional ensemble.

There is empirical evidence for this direction on credit-risk data and ROC-AUC benchmarks. There is not yet proof that it beats your tuned model on your exact 32 columns, so I’m separating the demonstrated results from the new experiment I think is worth attempting.

## 1. What the stronger evidence actually says

A. TabICLv2: a stronger pretrained tabular model

The ICML 2026 paper reports an average AUC rank of 3.31 across 120 binary-classification datasets in TALENT, compared with 4.62 for RealTabPFN-2.5 and 5.45 for TabPFN-2.5. Lower is better. On TabArena's binary tasks, TabICLv2 ranks second overall among the compared systems and is the strongest tabular foundation model in that evaluation.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



Evidence: [ICML 2026 paper](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v306/qu26a.html) · [Official implementation](https://github.com/soda-inria/tabicl)

B. Context selection: measured AUC gains on Home Credit

A recent credit-risk preprint compared five tabular foundation models across several context-selection strategies. On the full Home Credit dataset, balanced context selection achieved mean AUC 0.734 versus 0.703 with uniform selection—an absolute difference of 0.031 across the study's models and context sizes. At a 50,000-row balanced context, the reported AUC was 0.786 for TabPFN and 0.771 for TabICL.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



Evidence: [Data Presentation Over Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18635)

C. Localized TabICLv2: retrieval in a learned representation space

An August 2026 study compared retrieval using the model's learned row embeddings with retrieval directly from raw features. Across 30 binary tasks, mean AUC was 0.827 for fine-tuned embedding-space retrieval versus 0.817 for fine-tuned raw-feature retrieval. Full-context TabICLv2 scored 0.855, so localization was not better than the full model; its result was a more efficient approximation.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



This matters because your previous neighbour experiments used raw features or product-level smoothing. A model-learned similarity space is a materially different approach.

Evidence: [Localized TabICLv2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16429)

### The important caveat

The Home Credit context-selection study uses features derived from all seven original tables, and its classical baselines were not fully tuned for your competition. Its +0.031 result is a comparison between context strategies—not evidence of a +0.031 gain over your 0.772 incumbent.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



Separately, a credit-risk benchmark covering 14 probability-of-default datasets found average AUC of 0.7517 for TabICL, 0.7494 for CatBoost, and 0.7468 for LightGBM under its hyperparameter-optimization protocol. That is a modest real-world benchmark advantage, not a guarantee on this subset.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



## 2. The advanced experiment I would pursue next

The genuinely new move is to combine the two strongest mechanisms above into a class-balanced, query-specific context model that retrieves examples in TabICLv2's learned feature space.

This combined procedure has not been proven on your dataset. Its ingredients have published AUC evidence, which makes it a more defensible research hypothesis than another hand-engineered feature family.

## The proposed method

Your existing 32 application columns

Keep the decoded product signal and existing validated features

TabICLv2 learned representation

Learn a task-relevant notion of which applicants resemble one another

Retrieve balanced, relevant context

For each query, retrieve similar defaults and similar non-defaults separately in the learned space

Predict and evaluate ROC-AUC

Compare with full-context TabICLv2 and the incumbent LightGBM

Why this is different from your failed kNN and clustering experiments: it does not measure distance in raw input space and then smooth labels. It uses a pretrained model's learned representation to find relevant examples, then feeds those examples back through the model. The new localized-model study found that this learned-space retrieval outperformed raw-feature retrieval on mean AUC, although the full-context model remained stronger.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



The unproven extension: balance the retrieved context by class. For a first prototype, compare 32 positive and 32 negative neighbors per query against 64 of each class. Those sizes are experimental choices, not published optima. This combines the measured value of class balance with the measured value of learned-space retrieval, but the combined result must be tested from scratch.

## 3. A practical route to a real result

Don't launch all the models at once. Use a staged experiment so each run answers one clear question.

| Stage | Experiment                                                                                          | Decision                                                                   |
| ----- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1     | TabICLv2 using the training fold's full context                                                     | Does the newer model beat your current system on the restricted columns?   |
| 2     | TabICLv2 with an 18,000-row, 50:50 context: 9,000 defaults and 9,000 non-defaults                   | Does balancing its context improve ranking?                                |
| 3     | Average predictions across three independently sampled contexts for the winning context strategy    | Is its improvement stable to context selection?                            |
| 4     | If the model is promising, test balanced learned-space retrieval with the localized TabICLv2 method | Does query-specific selection improve beyond the simpler context approach? |

The 18,000-row context is chosen to be feasible with approximately 9,000 unique positive cases in an outer training fold. Verify the actual number of defaults in each fold before sampling; reduce the context size if necessary rather than duplicating examples silently. The paper shows that larger contexts can help TabPFN and TabICL, but it does not establish 18,000 as optimal for your data.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



Keep your product key, but avoid reusing a target-encoded feature that contains its own context row's label. Use your existing nested OOF procedure for training-row encodings, and derive validation encodings exclusively from the training fold. If the foundation model accepts the product key as categorical, retain that input too.

### The evaluation rule matters as much as the model

You've already run many experiments on the same folds, so I would freeze the configurations before starting. Use your existing folds for screening, with identical held-out rows for the incumbent and each candidate. Compare pooled OOF predictions using a paired, stratified bootstrap—not a t-test over five fold scores.

After screening, set aside a prospective confirmation subset before fitting the finalists and compare only the frozen incumbent and the selected candidate on it once. Since earlier experiments have already used the existing labels, this is not a perfectly untouched historical holdout, but it is still a useful prospective check against the new candidates.

Keep a candidate only if it produces a credible gain over your current ensemble. A more complicated model that scores the same or worse is not a win, regardless of how advanced its architecture sounds.

## My bottom line

The next move is not another 20-feature engineering search. It is a controlled trial of TabICLv2 with balanced context selection, followed—only if it shows promise—by learned-space, class-balanced retrieval.

The evidence is stronger than a speculative model suggestion: TabICLv2 has leading published AUC rankings across broad binary benchmarks, context selection has measured AUC effects on Home Credit, and learned-space retrieval has beaten raw-space retrieval on AUC.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+2



What the research cannot tell us is whether any of these will raise your 0.772 CV AUC. No study I found establishes that result on precisely your application-only 32-column subset against your tuned ensemble. The path to success is to test this specific, evidence-supported mechanism under a frozen evaluation—not to pretend the gain is already proven.
I found a substantially stronger research lead than another classifier blend: TabICLv2, a tabular foundation model published at ICML 2026. It was not on our earlier shortlist, and it has something the previous suggestions lacked: benchmark evidence specifically evaluating ROC-AUC, not just accuracy.

The authors evaluate it on 120 binary-classification datasets in the TALENT benchmark. Its reported average AUC rank is 3.31, compared with 4.62 for RealTabPFN-2.5 and 5.45 for TabPFN-2.5; lower ranks are better. On the separate TabArena benchmark, it ranks second across 24 binary-classification datasets. These are published benchmark rankings—not AUC gains measured on your Home Credit subset.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



## An even more relevant finding: context selection on Home Credit itself

I found a May 2026 research preprint that tests Home Credit Default Risk directly, comparing five tabular foundation models under seven ways of selecting their labeled context examples.

The paper reports these Home Credit results, averaged across the evaluated models and context sizes:

| Context-selection strategy           | Mean ROC-AUC |
| ------------------------------------ | ------------ |
| Uniform random sampling              | 0.703        |
| Balanced sampling                    | 0.734        |
| Hybrid balanced + diversity sampling | 0.732        |

That is a reported +0.031 ROC-AUC difference for balanced sampling versus uniform sampling in that study. The authors also report that increasing context size from 1,000 to 50,000 examples improved balanced and hybrid strategies by +0.034 and +0.045, respectively.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



This is much closer to the problem you are working on than a generic tabular benchmark. However, there's an important qualification: their Home Credit experiment uses features derived from all seven original tables, whereas your model is restricted to a 32-column application-only subset. Those numbers are evidence for a mechanism worth testing, not a forecast of your AUC gain.

## The experiment I would prioritise

The research points to a specific combination: TabICLv2 + class-balanced in-context examples. This combines the newer model's broad benchmark evidence with the direct Home Credit evidence for context selection. The combined method itself has not yet been demonstrated on your 32 columns, so it needs a controlled test.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



Why it's different from the experiments you've already described:

- This is not class weighting in LightGBM. The model is a pretrained tabular foundation model that predicts using labeled examples supplied as context.
- The context paper shows that changing which labeled examples the model sees can change its ROC-AUC materially—even with the same architecture.
- TabICLv2 automatically handles categorical columns and missing values. Its authors report that it can scale beyond its standard training-size range, although performance may degrade on larger contexts.&#x20;

  [image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

  GitHub

  +1



The official implementation is here: [TabICLv2 repository](https://github.com/soda-inria/tabicl).

### How I would test it without contaminating your evaluation

1. Lock a genuine holdout. Reserve 15% of the labeled training rows before any new candidate selection. Refit your current best ensemble on the remaining 85% so the incumbent and candidate are evaluated against the same holdout rows.
2. Run a controlled context comparison. Use your existing 32 columns and decoded product feature. With TabICLv2, compare two contexts of 10,000 unique rows sampled from the development data: one preserving the natural class proportions, and one containing 5,000 defaults and 5,000 non-defaults. No replacement sampling is needed at this context size.
3. Keep the product signal leakage-safe. Retain your validated product encoding only through the existing OOF/nested procedure. Fit all encodings using development labels alone; never use holdout labels to create features or select context rows.
4. Evaluate the prespecified candidates once. Record pooled AUC for the refitted incumbent, TabICLv2 with natural-proportion context, and TabICLv2 with balanced context. Estimate paired, stratified-bootstrap confidence intervals for the AUC differences. If you test a blend, determine its weight using development-only predictions—not holdout performance.

The 10,000-row context is a conservative starting point because the direct credit-risk study found useful results at 5,000–10,000 examples. It is not guaranteed to be optimal for your stronger incumbent.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org

+1



One rules check: TabICLv2's released checkpoint is pretrained on synthetic tabular tasks rather than requiring additional Home Credit records. Still, confirm that your course permits pretrained model weights; if it prohibits them, this candidate is not eligible.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://github.com\&sz=32)

GitHub

+1



## What I can and cannot claim

There is now genuine, published-in-a-preprint evidence of an AUC improvement from context selection on the Home Credit dataset: +0.031 in the paper's cross-model, cross-context average for balanced versus uniform sampling. That is a substantially stronger reason to run an experiment than a speculative claim that another booster might improve your score.&#x20;

[image](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://arxiv.org\&sz=32)

arxiv.org



But the paper uses features derived from all seven original tables and compares against classical models with default settings. It does not establish a +0.031 improvement against your tuned, application-only ensemble, and the newer TabICLv2-plus-balanced-context combination has not been tested on your exact subset.

That is the honest boundary of the evidence. This is the strongest new research-backed direction I found—not a promised score increase. The decisive result now has to come from the locked local holdout, not another literature-based AUC estimate.

I found one clear flaw in your gating and four likely opportunities, and I built them into a v13 notebook you can run on the best pipeline. I haven't run any of it on your real data, so there are no real numbers yet, and I can't promise a gain.

**Flaw: your significance gate rejects small real gains.**
- Your v10 NULL control (a baseline compared with itself) shows a 95% interval of about ±0.0006. No gain smaller than about 0.0006 can ever pass that gate.
- Your public history shows steps of +0.0002 to +0.0003 do transfer (E1 gained +0.0003, E2 +0.0002).
- So the product-aware "fail" (mean +0.00019, 4 of 5 folds positive) means inconclusive, not negative. Treat the interval as information, and gate on the nested mean gain plus fold positivity instead.
- v10's skipped cell 7 was by design, not an error. Both gates failed, as the notebook intended.

**What v11 told you.** Your notes put v6_B at 0.77836, so v11's 0.7784 is only +0.00004. Those family-bagged members added no information; it wasn't a low score caused by a bug.

**Other findings from reading your code and logs**
1. **Stale blend weights.** The weights (0.24 / 0.12 / 0.32 / 0.16 / 0.16) were fixed from the old members. The e1 members are now about +0.001 better each and nearly equal (0.7712 to 0.7718), so the best weights may have moved.
2. **Untried extra-trees.** `extra_trees=True` gained +0.0008 to +0.0014 per member in my held-out tests (core one-hot +0.00078, replicated at +0.00077; all-families one-hot +0.00139). It isn't in the e1 pipeline yet.
3. **Variant mixing is worth a lot.** Your logs show old 0.77202 and new 0.77258 mixing to 0.77330 (+0.0007). That makes an extra-trees variant a natural third component.
4. **View-stacking mismatch.** Training rows get view features from 3-fold inner models, while validation and test rows get models trained on all the fold's rows. With target encodings, 5 inner folds beat 3 in my test. This one is unproven here.
5. **Unequal seeds.** Two members use 5 full-data seeds and three use 3. Extra seeds cut test-side noise that held-out AUC can't show.

I found no label leakage in the view stacking or the learned-expectation features.

**`GCI_v13_reweight_and_ET_colab.ipynb`** is your reproduction notebook (it rebuilds your 0.77865 file exactly) plus two cells.
- **Cell 7 (about 5 minutes, no retraining).** It re-optimises the blend weights with nested CV and writes `v13_A_reweighted.csv`.
- **Cell 8 (about 1.7 hours).** It trains extra-trees versions of the five e1 members and shows each member next to its non-extra-trees twin. It then tests blend B (old + e1 + extra-trees) and blend C (swap e1 for extra-trees), and writes `v13_B…csv` and `v13_C…csv`.
- **Decision rule.** For both cells, submit only if the nested mean gain is at least +0.0002 and at least 4 of 5 folds are positive.
- **What I tested.** I smoke-tested both cells end to end on a small sample. The "PASS" and gain numbers in that test are meaningless, so ignore them.

I added all this to `research.md` as section 15.

I'd run cell 7 first, since it's cheap, and then cell 8. Tell me the printed results and we'll decide which file to submit. Neither is aimed at 0.79. That would need a discovery of the size of the q-fingerprint, and the weakness analysis in your notes found no learnable signal left in the residuals.
# GCI World 2026 - Research Notes (everything we learned)

Last updated after the q-fingerprint submission scored **0.77283** on the public leaderboard (previous best 0.76815).
This file is written so that you (or a new chat/assistant) can continue the competition without re-doing work. Facts are separated from guesses; every number says whether it is a
held-out (leak-free) estimate or a public-leaderboard score.

## 0. TL;DR
1. **The big discovery:** every "rate-curve shape" feature (rate_std, rate_range, r24, curvatures...) is a function of ONE number, `q = AMT_ANNUITY / AMT_CREDIT`.
   The real signal is default risk as a function of `q` at fine resolution (about 800-1,500 distinct values = lender loan products). Trees cannot resolve it with default binning.
   Giving LightGBM smoothed, leak-safe target encodings of `q` (rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals) lifted a single model from 0.76265 to 0.76713 held-out, and the public score from 0.76815 to **0.77283**.
2. The held-out method (below) has predicted every public move to within about 0.0003 - trust it, not local numbers from weak models or the rounded public board.
3. Everything that is a re-expression of columns the trees already see (ratios, logs, EXT divisions, harmonic means, exact rates) is worth ~0. Gains come from information trees cannot learn: fine-resolution product identity (q), exact loan templates, more rows/seeds, diverse models.
4. 0.785 would be +0.012 over 0.77283 (about 2.6 times what the q discovery added). Nothing found so far supports promising it; the best chance is hunting for a second "hidden structure" like q (section 9).
5. Rules: only the supplied train/test/sample files. No external data/labels, no hand-labeling, no test labels. Final ranking uses the private leaderboard and your LAST submitted file. Honors/Outstanding needs code that reproduces the CSV, and AI-assisted code must be disclosed.

## 3. Data facts
- 32 predictors: 24 numeric + 8 categorical (NAME_CONTRACT_TYPE, CODE_GENDER, NAME_INCOME_TYPE, NAME_EDUCATION_TYPE, NAME_FAMILY_STATUS, NAME_HOUSING_TYPE, OCCUPATION_TYPE, ORGANIZATION_TYPE). Default rate 8.07%.
- SK_ID_CURR was re-indexed: train 0-171,201, test 171,202-232,701 (no leak in the ID; row order carries no signal, AUC 0.503). No duplicate rows within or across train/test.
- DAYS_EMPLOYED = 365243 is a sentinel (18% of train rows; default 5.4% vs 8.7%). Missingness: EXT_SOURCE_1 69.5%, OWN_CAR_AGE 66%, EXT_SOURCE_3 31.9%; missingness itself carries signal.
- No major train/test shift (PSI < 0.005 on top features; adversarial AUC about 0.516). EXT_SOURCE_1/2/3 are the strongest raw features (univariate AUC 0.66-0.68).
- Only 0.3% of rows share person-level fields (birth, registration, ID dates); the same-person link is real (0.88 AUC inside groups) but covers too few rows to matter (+0.0001).

## 4. The q-fingerprint discovery (details)
**Why it works.** For an amortizing loan, ANNUITY/CREDIT = r(1+r)^n / ((1+r)^n - 1). For any fixed term n, the implied rate is a strictly increasing function of q = ANNUITY/CREDIT, so all rate/curvature/dispersion features computed from q are functions of q alone
(R^2 of each shape feature against a 400-bin function of q: 0.9993-1.0000). They add nothing to a tree that already has q (the exact-rate features alone gave +0.0002 when models were trained properly).
What matters is that **risk varies sharply across specific q values**: q rounded to 4 decimals has about 814 distinct values and every row shares its value with at least 5 others - lender products (rate x term combinations).
LightGBM's default 255 bins and regularization cannot resolve this; a smoothed lookup of default rate by q scores 0.675 AUC alone (earlier exact-value fingerprints ~0.62).
**How it is built (leak-safe).** For each key (q rounded to 3, 4, 5 decimals): TE = (sum_y + m0 * prior) / (count + m0) with m0 = 100, 30, 30, plus the count. Training rows use inner 5-fold out-of-fold encodings (seed 7);
validation/test rows use all rows of the training portion only. Outer folds: StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle, seed 42).
**Evidence (all held-out, same folds).**
- As a blend member only: +0.00125 at 10% weight (+0.00111 at 7%, +0.00101 at 15%, -0.0034 at 30%); choosing the best member on 4 folds and scoring the 5th: +0.00118, 5/5 folds positive.
- As FEATURES inside LightGBM (the strong version): 0.76713 vs 0.76265 without (+0.00448). Features beat the blend by 4x.
- Three models: qf_onehot 0.76713, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678, qf_mono 0.76746; average 0.76843 (previous best recipe 0.76458); recipe + average at W=0.7: 0.76861 (+0.00403); W chosen on 4 folds scored on the 5th: +0.00408, 5/5 positive; optimum flat for W in 0.7-1.0.
- Resolution matters: 182 distinct values +0.00102 (blend test); 814 values +0.00125; 1,493 values +0.00127. Shrinkage does not (m0 10-60 all about +0.0012).
- Other keys as extra features gave nothing (first 4 of 5 folds, change vs q-only: +0.00009, +0.00008, -0.00014, -0.00052): goods/credit (3 and 4 decimals), credit/income, annuity/income, q x goods ratio, q x contract type. q is special.
- As blend members, only q x contract type added anything among 12 customer states; the others hurt.
**Prediction vs reality.** Held-out predicted +0.0040; public gave +0.0047 (0.76815 -> 0.77283).
**Why the rate-shape/RD3 chain only moved +0.0001-0.0002 per step:** it was a coarse, 1-D, weak-anchored approximation of this signal, applied as a tiny blend.

## 5. Validation method (use this, and its traps)
- Held-out = every training row predicted by a model that never saw it (5-fold, seed 42). Fixed boosting rounds chosen WITHOUT the held-out labels (early stopping on the held-out fold is optimistic; it inflated an early pseudo-label "gain" to +0.0008 when the clean result was -0.0014).
- Target-derived features must be nested (inner out-of-fold for training rows). Never use in-sample predictions to build residuals.
- A correction fitted against a WEAK anchor model does not transfer: RD3 (anchor weaker than the champion), model-residual templates (fast base 0.759), EXT-division/rate models with under-trained baselines all showed "+0.003" locally and ~0 or negative publicly/against a strong base.
- A pre-registered test: choose weights/members on 4 folds, score on the 5th, repeat; need 5/5 positive. Public noise (0.0001-0.0005) is larger than most single ideas - decide with held-out, confirm with ONE public submission.
- test.csv has no labels; only the leaderboard scores test predictions. Do not loop on the public board.

## 6. Complete experiment log (held-out unless noted)
**Feature families (3-fold LightGBM):** base 32 raw 0.75185; +clean flags 0.75141; +EXT aggregates 0.75050; +financial ratios 0.75958 (+0.0077, the big early win); +age/employment 0.75114; +bureau 0.75143; +count encodings 0.75165; +group means 0.75131. Final 67-feature set = clean + fin + age + bureau.
**Model families (5-fold OOF):** monotone LGB 0.76238, plain 0.76221, shallow 0.76230, XGB 0.76169, GAM (splines) 0.74801, extra-trees LGB 0.75683; blends 0.7628-0.7629 (models agree 99%).
**More rows:** learning curve 38.5k/77k/115.5k/154k rows -> 0.75104/0.75795/0.76092/0.76387 (about +0.003 per 38k rows). 5-fold -> 10-fold -> full-data refit each helped. **More seeds:** 1 seed 0.7616 -> 3 0.7624 -> 5 0.7627 -> 8 0.7633.
**10-fold:** monotone 0.76366, shallow 0.76332, one-hot 0.76344, XGB 0.76179; blends 0.76381 (3 models), 0.76405 (+one-hot). **Leak-free 5-fold:** one-hot 0.76265, monotone 0.76219, shallow 0.76209, one-hot all-families 0.76145, all-families 0.76099; recipes 0.76312 (core) -> 0.76367 (file 13) -> 0.76416 (file 15) -> 0.76458 (+residuals).
**Diversity blends:** all-families LGB (130 features: EXT aggregates, count encodings, group differences) is weak alone but blends well (+0.0004 at 20%); XGB slightly hurts the blend; high-capacity LGB, one-hot XGB, native categoricals, top-40 features: nothing.
**Loan-template fingerprints/residuals:** exact (income, annuity, credit, goods) hierarchy fingerprint alone 0.62148, blend +0.00045 at 5%; template-own-risk residual (exact credit/annuity [CA] and with goods [CAG] x contract/housing/employment sentinel): 48 combos screened, best single +0.00036, average of top 6 +0.00042 at 5% (5/5 folds; public +0.0002); 189-candidate extended screen and model-residual versions: 0.
**Rejected after clean tests:** pseudo-labelling (-0.0014); exact-rate features (+0.00023); EXT divisions + rate (0.76165 vs 0.76288); harmonic EXT + loan-to-value (-0.00024 alone, blend +0.0001); max_bin=511 (+0.0002 alone); weighted loss (0); KNN target rate (-0.002); target encoding of categoricals; MLP/NN (sklearn, 0.736-0.745 alone, blend <= 0); same-person feature (+0.0001); CatBoost (too slow here; ChatGPT's 1-5% blends were 0 to -0.00007 publicly).
**Research suggesting NNs:** RealMLP/TabM with tuning+ensembling can match GBDTs and blend well on 1K-500K rows (Holzmueller et al., NeurIPS 2024 https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04491; TabArena https://alphaxiv.org/abs/2506.16791). Not tested here (no torch/GPU): see notebook section 4.
**Leaderboard context (table you shared before the q discovery):** rank 15 was 0.77727, rank 10 0.77802, rank 1 0.79101; you were around rank 66-70 at 0.768. Use the current board; scores above 0.777 on these 32 columns are far beyond what any held-out test of ours supported before q.

## 7. Final architecture (the file that scored 0.77283)
final = 0.30 x GCI_submission_residual.csv + 0.70 x mean rank of three q-feature LightGBM models.
- GCI_submission_residual.csv = 0.95 x file 15 + 0.05 x mean rank of six template-residual scores [CAGxCONTRACT, CAxCONTRACT, CAGxHOUSING, CAxHOUSING, CAxDE_SENT, CAGxDE_SENT]; file 15 = weights 12% monotone LGB, 12% shallow LGB, 36% one-hot LGB, 16% all-families LGB, 20% one-hot all-families LGB, 4% exact-value fingerprint.
- Each q-model member = 50% average of 5 fold-models + 50% average of 3 full-data seeds (ranks). Parameters and rounds are in `GCI_q_fingerprint_colab.ipynb` (and `GCI_qfingerprint_submission.py`).
- Verified reproducible: the script's full-data predictions for qf_allfam_oh and qf_mono equal the saved ones exactly; the full pipeline was not re-run end-to-end in one go.

## 8. Files (outputs folder)
`submit/GCI_submission_qfingerprint.csv` (0.77283), `submit/GCI_qfingerprint_submission.py`, `submit/GCI_submission_residual.csv` (0.76815), `submit/GCI_solution_final.py`, `submit/GCI_residual_submission.py`, `submit/README_FINAL.md`,
`final/` (older scripts and files 07-15), `colab/GCI_q_fingerprint_colab.ipynb` (self-contained Colab pipeline), `colab/research.md` (this file).

## 9. Research plan beyond 0.77283 (ranked; every item untested unless stated)
Use the notebook's `test_extra_key` (5-fold, leak-free, ~10 min) to test candidates; only a gain above ~+0.0003 with the same folds is worth a public submission.
1. **Hunt for a second "hidden structure" like q.** q worked because lenders generate loan terms by formula. Look for other values generated by formulas/catalogs: exact AMT_CREDIT, AMT_GOODS_PRICE, AMT_ANNUITY, AMT_INCOME_TOTAL values as keys (as FEATURES, not blends); round-number patterns (value mod 1,000 / 5,000 / 9,000, last digits, multiples of 4.5); annuity per month vs goods price; DAYS_BIRTH as a key.
2. **Re-do the whole ensemble with q-features inside every member** (shallow LGB, extra seeds, XGBoost, CatBoost) and re-run the 25-step ensemble search; then re-test the residual-template and fingerprint blends against this stronger base (they were tuned against a weaker one and may now overlap).
3. **Tune the q-models now that they carry more information:** more rounds/leaves, lower learning rate, 10-fold, 8+ seeds, full-data refits (the seed and learning curves say +0.001 or more).
4. **Pairwise error analysis against the NEW champion** with strict out-of-fold predictions (notebook gives OOF): where does the champion rank positives below negatives? Group by q bin, contract type, EXT missingness, product groups.
5. **Higher-resolution q keys** (round 6 decimals; hierarchical backoff) and q-conditioned encodings as features for sub-populations (contract type was the only helpful state as a blend; others hurt).
6. **NN diversity (RealMLP/TabM) on Colab GPU with the q-features** - earlier NNs were weak (0.745) and blend-negative; low expectation, listed for completeness.
7. Do NOT repeat: harmonic EXT, exact-rate/EXT-division features, pseudo-labelling, adaptive gating, KNN residuals, global meta-models, weighted loss, extra ratio keys (goods/credit, credit/income, annuity/income).
**Honest expectation:** 0.785 needs about +0.012. The q discovery was worth +0.0047 and came from asking "what structure generates these numbers?". Another discovery of similar size is possible but not guaranteed; do not plan around it.

## 12. Update: product-key decode (your 0.77715 notebook), verification, and what is left
**Scores added to the ledger:** the Colab notebook output (3 q-feature LightGBM models + our blend) **0.77321**; the product-key notebook blend **0.77715** (+0.0039). Held-out numbers in the product-key notebook as reported by you
(not recomputed by me except the check below): qf_onehot 0.76713 -> 0.76992, qf_allfam_oh 0.76678 -> 0.77070, qf_mono 0.76746 -> 0.77035, 3-member average 0.76843 -> 0.77158.
**The decode (label-free):** annuity = Q0 x credit + F, where F is a fixed monthly fee (about 129.5; a plateau of 125.5-130.5 scores the same) on about 75.1% of cash loans. A row is flagged as fee-bearing when (annuity - 129.5)/credit has more rows sharing its 5-decimal value than annuity/credit does.
Q0 = fee-adjusted ratio for flagged rows, q otherwise; keys P3/P4/P5 (Q0 rounded to 3/4/5 decimals) and PF4 (Q0 4 decimals + fee flag) are target-encoded exactly like the q keys (nested, smoothed).
**My independent check (fast harness: core one-hot LightGBM, lr 0.08, 450 rounds, 5-fold, same folds; use for relative comparisons only):** q keys only 0.76680 -> q + product keys **0.76987 (+0.00307)**. This confirms the notebook's gain.
**Coverage:** after the decode 99.1% of cash loans and 99.9% of revolving loans sit on a product value shared by >= 20 rows. Only 1,987 cash rows (0.9%) are unexplained and their best second fee value is the same ~127.5 -> there is no second annuity-fee layer to find.
**Credit vs goods price has a formula too, but it carries no extra risk signal:** 28.4% of cash loans have credit == goods; otherwise (credit - goods)/goods = k x 0.0132 with integer k (share of all rows: k=0 34.9%, 15: 8.4%, 12: 7.7%, 9: 6.6%, 10: 5.0%, 16: 4.5%, 8: 4.0%, 24: 3.3%; looks like insurance options).
As a key or joint key with the product it did NOT help: + k class 0.76960, + product x k 0.76925, + all joint keys 0.76907 (reference 0.76987).
**Other negative tests this round (same harness, reference 0.76987 unless noted):** numeric decoded features (Q0, fee flag, net annuity, term, net annuity/income) 0.76961; coarse backoff key (Q0 at 2 decimals) 0.76960; pooled train+test product counts 0.76972.
**Fast-harness screens against the q-only baseline 0.76680 (all keys as nested features):** exact AMT_CREDIT 0.76692 (+0.00012); exact goods price 0.76621; exact annuity 0.76640; exact income 0.76665; credit+goods pair 0.76654; DAYS_BIRTH 0.76610; fee (credit-goods) 0.76663;
q at 6 decimals 0.76662; 15 round-number/modulo features 0.76566. Nothing else behaves like q/product.
**Model capacity with fine keys (same harness, base = 11 leaves, min child 150, 450 rounds = 0.76680):** 31 leaves 0.76273; min child 50 0.76603; 31 leaves + min child 50 0.76294; 15 leaves + min child 100 + feature fraction 0.7 0.76529; 7 leaves 0.76650; 300 rounds 0.76678; 700 rounds 0.76616.
The noisy fine-resolution encodings make flexible trees overfit: keep the notebook's settings, do not add capacity. (A more-regularised direction - min child 300/600, stronger L2, lower feature fraction - was prepared but not run.)
**What this means:** the structure in annuity/credit/goods is now decoded as far as the data allows; the product keys are saturated (every extra view of them added <= 0, within noise).
**Remaining levers (all small, honest expectation 0.77715 -> about 0.778):** (1) choose the LGB/HGB blend weight from held-out predictions, not from the public board (compute the HGB model's OOF with the same folds; the shared HGB test array has no OOF in the notebook);
(2) product keys inside EVERY member (shallow LGB, XGB, CatBoost, extra seeds, 10-fold) and a fresh ensemble search; (3) re-test the template-residual blends on top of the new base (they were tuned on a weaker one); (4) the regularisation direction above.
0.78 needs +0.0029 from 0.77715; it is plausible only if several of these stack; it is not guaranteed.

Decision rule that worked: choose weights on 4 folds, score the 5th, require >= 4/5 positive folds before spending a submission. The ensemble is saturated: four extra member types each moved the held-out blend by a few ten-thousandths or less.
**Inner folds for the nested target encodings (training rows use K inner folds; held-out/test rows use all fold-train rows).** Hypothesis: more inner folds shrink the train/test mismatch. Result (fast harness, q + product keys, 5 outer folds): K=2 0.76937, K=3 0.76946, K=4 0.76928, **K=5 0.76987**, K=10 0.76931, K=20 0.76868.
More inner folds are WORSE (K=20: -0.0012), probably the leave-one-out effect (each row's encoding excludes its own label, which trees can exploit); the curve is flat/noisy between 2 and 10. Keep K = 5.

**Findings (code and design):**
1. *The bootstrap gate is underpowered.* The NULL control shows the 95% interval is about +-0.0006 wide, so no gain below ~0.0006 can pass, although public steps of +0.0002 to +0.0003 do show up (E1 +0.0003, E2 +0.0002). A fail at +0.0002 means "inconclusive", not "negative". Use the nested mean + fold-positivity rule and treat the interval as information.
2. *Blend weights are stale.* The five weights (0.24/0.12/0.32/0.16/0.16) were fixed from the OLD members; the e1 members are now about +0.001 better each and nearly equal (0.7712-0.7718), so the optimum may differ. Cell 7 of v13 re-optimises them with nested CV from cached predictions (no retraining).
3. *Untested lever: extra_trees (+0.0008 to +0.0014 per member in my LightGBM held-out tests)* has not been applied to the e1 pipeline; since mixing variants was worth +0.0007, an extra_trees variant of the e1 members is the natural third component (cell 8 of v13).
4. *View-stacking train/test mismatch (not tested):* view features for training rows come from 3-fold inner models (about 53% of the rows) while validation/test rows get models trained on 100% of the fold-train rows. With target encodings, 5 inner folds beat 3 (0.76987 vs 0.76946) and 2-20 inner folds were all within noise to worse, so this is a small, unproven opportunity (try 5 inner folds in compute_views).
5. *Unequal full-data seeds:* qf_shallow and qf_reg use 5 seeds, the other three 3 seeds. More seeds reduce test-side noise that the held-out AUC cannot show (held-out uses one model per fold); +0.0002 to +0.0005 is plausible, unmeasured.
6. No leakage found in the view stacking (views for validation rows come from models trained on the fold-train rows only) or in the learned-expectation features (label-free).
**v13 (GCI_v13_reweight_and_ET_colab.ipynb):** your reproduction notebook + cell 7 (nested weight re-optimisation, ~5 min) + cell 8 (extra_trees e1 members, ~1.7 h, three-way blends B and C). Smoke-tested end to end on small data; no real numbers yet.
